is strictly monotonic, it simply provides a regraduation of the degrees of belief
\xi
. Any legitimate inference which satisfies the associativity constraint can be represented by the equation above. Thus, one can then adopt the simplest representative by choosing
\phi
equal to the identity,
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{\Xi}
, so that,

\xi^{(\mathrm{Id}_{\Xi})}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(b|\Gamma),

(2.43)

which makes
f_{\vee}
linear in its first two arguments. Of course this choice gives the impression that we are really after the sum rule, however we could have also picked the monotonic function
\phi=\log
, which gives,

\xi^{(\log)}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)\xi(b|\Gamma).

(2.44)

This representation also constrains
f_{\vee}
to be linear in its first two arguments
25
25

25

Any scaling of
\phi=\log
to
\phi=\alpha\log
where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, results in

\xi^{(\alpha\log)}(a\vee b|\Gamma)^{\alpha}=\xi(a|\Gamma)^{\alpha}\xi(b|\Gamma)^{\alpha},

(2.45)

which is the general result obtained in
[
33
]
for the product rule, but which is no longer linear.
.

Before settling on a choice of
\phi
, it will be useful to list a set of special cases. First, recalling
a\vee\mathbf{F}\Leftrightarrow a
(
A.16
) we have,

\displaystyle\xi(a\vee\mathbf{F}|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(\mathbf{F}|\Gamma),\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}}\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\xi(a|\Gamma).

(2.46)

Due to commutativity of
\vee
we also have that,

f_{\vee}(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi(a|\Gamma))=\xi(a|\Gamma),

(2.47)

Inserting (
2.47
) into (
2.42
) we find,

\phi(\xi(a\vee\mathbf{F}|\Gamma))=\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))+\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}}),

(2.48)

however
\xi(a\vee\mathbf{F}|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)
, hence we must have,

\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=0.

(2.49)

Thus, while the function
\phi
may be arbitrary, its evaluation of
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
is not
26
26

26

This can also be traced back to the definition of
\psi
(
B.42
) which explicitly defined
\psi(0)=\phi^{-1}(0)=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
.
. Thus, for any choice of
\phi
, the value of
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
is constrained so that (
2.49
) holds. This can also be demonstrated from the identity
\mathbf{F}\vee\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}
,

\phi(\xi(\mathbf{F}\vee\mathbf{T}|\Gamma))=\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})+\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})=\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}}).

(2.50)

A convenient regraduation —

Without loss of generality, one can always recast the function
\xi
as the composition,
\xi^{\prime}=\phi\circ\xi
. Since
\xi
is continuous, as well as
\phi
, then the composition of maps
\phi\circ\xi
is also continuous. Likewise, since
\phi
is monotonic, then the transitivity property (
2.7
) is also preserved under the composition of
\xi
with
\phi
. Thus, we can define the
regraduated
degree of belief,

\xi^{\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\phi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)),

(2.51)

so that we get explicitly the sum rule,

\xi^{\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)+\xi^{\prime}(b|\Gamma).

(2.52)

With this choice, we have left open the functional form of
\xi^{\prime}
as it applies to statements in
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. The sum above does not inform one as to what function
\xi^{\prime}
, or equivalently
\phi
, in
C^{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
that one should use as a degree of belief. It is strictly a rule for manipulating degrees of belief
\xi^{\prime}
. If one accepts the axioms
1.5
,
1.7
and
1.1
, then the general solution must be (
2.51
). The space
\Xi^{\prime}
then, is simply the result of a homeomorphism
\phi:\Xi\rightarrow\Xi^{\prime}
, so that
\Xi^{\prime}\subset C^{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
and

\Xi^{\prime}\cong_{\mathrm{homeo}}\Xi,

(2.53)

From here we can identify
\phi\circ f_{\vee}=f_{\vee}^{\prime}
, where
f_{\vee}^{\prime}
is simply addition on
\Xi^{\prime}
. We then have the following commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\vee}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}\times\Xi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\vee}^{\prime}}

Figure 7:
Commutative diagram for the sum rule of two mutually exclusive propositions.

For mutually exclusive propositions, the diagram above commutes,

f_{\vee}^{\prime}\circ(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\xi^{\prime}\circ\vee

(2.54)

The negation function
f_{\neg}
—

Using the fact that
a\wedge\neg a|\Gamma\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{F}
, and
a\vee\neg a|\Gamma\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{T}
, we also have

\forall a|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:\xi^{\prime}(a\vee\neg a|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)+\xi^{\prime}(\neg a|\Gamma)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}},

(2.55)

which, according to (
2.4
), means that for any degree of belief
\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)
,

\xi^{\prime}(\neg a|\Gamma)=f_{\neg}^{\prime}(\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma))=\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{T}}-\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma).

(2.56)

At this point it will be beneficial to drop the primes from the degrees of belief
\xi^{\prime}
for brevity, although one should not confuse the
\xi
’s from here on out with the
\xi
’s from before the regraduation.

The general sum rule —

To determine the general sum rule for arbitrary statements,
a|\Gamma
and
b|\Gamma
, we can use the distributive property of conjunction. Since any proposition can be written as the disjunction of two mutually exclusive statements,

a|\Gamma\Leftrightarrow(a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(a|\Gamma\wedge\neg b|\Gamma),

(2.57)

we have that,

\displaystyle a|\Gamma\vee b|\Gamma

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\left[\frac{}{}(a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(a|\Gamma\wedge\neg b|\Gamma)\right]\vee\left[\frac{}{}(a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(\neg a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\right]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\left[\frac{}{}(a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(\neg a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(a|\Gamma\wedge\neg b|\Gamma)\right]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a|\Gamma\vee[\neg a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma].

(2.58)

Since
a|\Gamma
and
[\neg a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma]
are mutually exclusive, we have,

\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi(a\vee(\neg a\wedge b)|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(\neg a\wedge b|\Gamma)

(2.59)

Adding and subtracting
\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)
to both sides yields,

\displaystyle\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(\neg a\wedge b|\Gamma)+\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\left[\frac{}{}\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)+\xi(\neg a\wedge b|\Gamma)\right]-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma).

(2.60)

Then, using the fact that
(a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)
and
(\neg a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma)
are mutually exclusive we get,

\displaystyle\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi((a\wedge b)\vee(\neg a\wedge b)|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(b|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma),

(2.61)

which is the general sum rule. The general sum rule must also be associative, which one can easily check by evaluating the disjunction in two ways. First,

\displaystyle\forall a|\Gamma,b|\Gamma,c|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:\xi(a\vee(b\vee c)|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(b\vee c|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge(b\vee c)|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(b|\Gamma)+\xi(c|\Gamma)-\xi(b\wedge c|\Gamma)

\displaystyle-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge c|\Gamma)+\xi(a\wedge b\wedge c|\Gamma),

(2.62)

where we used the distributive property for
\xi(a\wedge(b\vee c)|\Gamma)=\xi([a\wedge b]\vee[a\wedge c]|\Gamma)
. We also have that,

\displaystyle\forall a|\Gamma,b|\Gamma,c|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:\xi((a\vee b)\vee c|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)+\xi(c|\Gamma)-\xi((a\vee b)\wedge c)|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\xi(a|\Gamma)+\xi(b|\Gamma)+\xi(c|\Gamma)-\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle-\xi(a\wedge c|\Gamma)-\xi(b\wedge c|\Gamma)+\xi(a\wedge b\wedge c|\Gamma),

(2.63)

so that in general,

\xi(a\vee(b\vee c)|\Gamma)=\xi((a\vee b)\vee c|\Gamma),

(2.64)

as expected.

2.8
Representation of the conjunction
f_{\wedge}

There are several ways in which we could determine the functional form of
f_{\wedge}
. No matter which way we choose however, the result must be consistent with what we already know about the form of
f_{\vee}
and
f_{\neg}
, since the three are related through (
2.27
) and (
2.28
). In Cox’s original derivation
[
33
]
, he used the associativity constraint applied to
f_{\wedge}
, which of course leads to the same solution as in (
2.42
). It is perfectly reasonable to take this approach, however one must constrain the choice of the function
\phi
so as not to conflict with the choice for
f_{\vee}
. In other words, one cannot independently determine that the functional form of
f_{\wedge}
is a sum, otherwise they violate (
2.27
) and (
2.28
). We will discuss more about these results in Section
1.5
.

In order to be consistent with the chosen regraduation of (
2.42
), we will use the distributive property of
\wedge
and
\vee
(
A.20
). This will incorporate directly the already chosen regraduation of
\phi
. To see this, consider the statement,

\forall a|\Gamma,b|\Gamma,c|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:a|\Gamma\wedge[b|\Gamma\vee c|\Gamma]\Leftrightarrow[a|\Gamma\wedge b|\Gamma]\vee[a|\Gamma\wedge c|\Gamma].

(2.65)

Written using the properties (
A.20
) we have,

\forall a|\Gamma,b|\Gamma,c|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}:a\wedge(b\vee c)|\Gamma\Leftrightarrow[a\wedge b]\vee[a\wedge c]|\Gamma.

(2.66)

Now consider the degree of belief in the conjunction,

\xi(a\wedge(b\vee c)|\Gamma)=f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b\vee c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right).

(2.67)

Due to the distributivity property this is also equal to,

\displaystyle f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b\vee c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)+\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)

\displaystyle+f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right).

(2.68)

This is a functional equation for the three quantities
\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)
and
\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)
. This functional equation, which is a multi-variate version of Cauchy’s functional equation
[
38
]
, demonstrates that
f_{\wedge}
must be linear in its second argument,

Theorem 2.2

(Cauchy’s functional equation)
.

Let
f_{\wedge}:\Xi\times\Xi\rightarrow\Xi
be an additive function in its second argument (
2.68
). Then,
f_{\wedge}
must be linear in its second argument. The general solution is,

\forall\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\in\Xi:f_{\wedge}(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a))=\lambda g_{\wedge}(\xi(a|\Gamma))\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a),

(2.69)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

A proof for (
2.69
) is given in
Appendix B
. From here we can consider the commutative property of
\wedge
,
a\wedge b=b\wedge a
, so that

f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)=f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(b|\Gamma),\xi(a|\Gamma\wedge b)\right),

(2.70)

which using (
2.69
) is written,

g_{\wedge}(\xi(a|\Gamma))\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)=g_{\wedge}(\xi(b|\Gamma))\xi(a|\Gamma\wedge b).

(2.71)

If we consider the special case for when
a|\Gamma
and
b|\Gamma
are independent so that

\xi(a|\Gamma\wedge b)=\xi(a|\Gamma)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)=\xi(b|\Gamma),

(2.72)

then we have for (
2.71
),

g_{\wedge}(\xi(a|\Gamma))\xi(b|\Gamma)=g_{\wedge}(\xi(b|\Gamma))\xi(a|\Gamma).

(2.73)

Thus the function
g_{\wedge}
must also be linear so that in general we have,

f_{\wedge}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)=\lambda^{\prime}\xi(a|\Gamma)\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a).

(2.74)

The arbitrary constant
\lambda^{\prime}
can be set by regraduating
\xi
again using the property
\xi(a\wedge\mathbf{T}|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)
,

\xi(a\wedge\mathbf{T}|\Gamma)=\lambda\xi(a|\Gamma)\xi_{\mathbf{T}}=\xi(a|\Gamma).

(2.75)

Thus we must have
\lambda=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}^{-1}
,

\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\frac{1}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\xi(a|\Gamma)\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a).

(2.76)

Multiplying through by
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}^{-1}
,

\frac{\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}=\frac{\xi(a|\Gamma)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\frac{\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}.

(2.77)

Thus, we can define
\xi^{\prime}=\xi/\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
so that we recover the product rule for conjunction,

\xi^{\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)\xi^{\prime}(b|\Gamma\wedge a).

(2.78)

2.9
Representations using
C^{\infty}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})

Let us assume instead that the space of functions
\Xi
belongs to the other extreme,
\Xi\subset C^{\infty}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
. Using the tools of calculus we can derive the same sum and product rules from the previous sections. The derivation here follows along the same lines as the one first achieved by Cox
[
27
]
and later improved by others
[
26
,
25
]
. We begin again with the derivation of the sum rule for mutually exclusive propositions,

\displaystyle\xi(a\vee b\vee c|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma)\right),\xi(c|\Gamma)\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(a|\Gamma),f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}\xi(b|\Gamma),\xi(c|\Gamma)\right)\right).

(2.79)

To simplify the derivation, we adopt the notation,

\xi(a|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a,\quad\xi(b|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}b,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\xi(c|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}c.

(2.80)

Then, the associativity equation becomes,

f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(a,b),c)=f_{\vee}(a,f_{\vee}(b,c)).

(2.81)

To probe the properties of this equation, we will take various derivatives up to order two, although we assume that the functions
\xi
are smooth. Since there are four different versions of the function
f_{\vee}
above, we will introduce an additional notation to simplify future equations,

f_{\vee}(a,b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{ab}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad f_{\vee}(b,c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{bc}.

(2.82)

Then the associativity equation simplifies further,

f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)=f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc}).

(2.83)

Taking the derivative with respect to
a,b
and
c
we find,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathfrak{ab}}f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc}),

(2.84)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathfrak{ab}}f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathfrak{bc}}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c),

(2.85)

\displaystyle\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathfrak{bc}}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c).

(2.86)

One observation is that the derivatives of
f_{\vee}
with respect to either the first or second argument is always positive. The reason is due to axiom (
2.7
), which tells us that

\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)\geq 0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)\geq 0.

(2.87)

Dividing the second equation (
2.85
) by the first (
2.84
) we find,

\frac{\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)}{\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)}=\frac{\partial_{\mathfrak{bc}}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})}{\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})}\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c).

(2.88)

Likewise, dividing the second equation by the third (
2.86
) gives,

\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)\frac{\partial_{\mathfrak{ab}}f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)}{\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(\mathfrak{ab},c)}=\frac{\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c)}{\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c)}.

(2.89)

We can introduce another convenient notation to simplify the ratios in the following equations. Consider the definition,

K(a,b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)}{\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)},

(2.90)

so that the ratios,

\frac{\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)}{\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)}=K(a,b),\quad\frac{\partial_{\mathfrak{bc}}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})}{\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,\mathfrak{bc})}=K(a,\mathfrak{bc}),\quad\frac{\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c)}{\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c)}=K(b,c).

(2.91)

The right hand side of (
2.88
) contains a term proportional to the derivative of
f_{\vee}(b,c)
with respect to
b
. If we multiply both sides of (
2.88
) by
K(b,c)
, the right hand side of (
2.88
) will then be proportional to the derivative of
f_{\vee}(b,c)
, but now with respect to
c
,

\displaystyle K(a,b)

\displaystyle=K(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c),

(2.92)

\displaystyle K(a,b)K(b,c)

\displaystyle=K(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c).

(2.93)

One can easily verify that taking the derivative of (
2.93
) with respect to
b
is equivalent to taking the derivative of (
2.92
) with respect to
c
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\left[K(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c)\right]=\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\left[K(a,\mathfrak{bc})\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c)\right]

(2.94)

But, the right hand side of (
2.94
) is zero due to the left hand side of (
2.92
),

\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\left[K(a,b)\right]=0.

(2.95)

Thus, the derivative of the left hand side of (
2.93
) with respect to
b
must also be zero,

\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\left[K(a,b)K(b,c)\right]=0,

(2.96)

which leads to,

\frac{1}{K(a,b)}\frac{\partial K(a,b)}{\partial b}=-\frac{1}{K(b,c)}\frac{\partial K(b,c)}{\partial b}.

(2.97)

Since the left hand side is independent of
c
while the right hand side is independent of
a
, both sides must only depend on
b
, i.e.,

\frac{1}{K(a,b)}\frac{\partial K(a,b)}{\partial b}=k(b),\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\frac{1}{K(b,c)}\frac{\partial K(b,c)}{\partial b}=-k(b).

(2.98)

Since both
\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(a,b)
and
\partial_{a}f_{\vee}(a,b)
are positive (
2.87
), we can integrate both equations in (
2.98
) to get,

\displaystyle K(a,b)

\displaystyle=K(a,0)\exp\left[\int_{0}^{b}db^{\prime}\,k(b^{\prime})\right],

(2.99)

\displaystyle K(b,c)

\displaystyle=K(0,c)\exp\left[-\int_{0}^{b}db^{\prime}\,k(b^{\prime})\right].

(2.100)

Defining the term,

\mathcal{K}(b)=\exp\left[-\int_{0}^{b}db^{\prime}\,k(b^{\prime})\right],

(2.101)

we find from (
2.99
) and (
2.100
) that,

K(a,b)=K(a,0)\mathcal{K}(b)^{-1}=K(0,b)\mathcal{K}(a).

(2.102)

So,
K(a,b)
can be expressed either with respect to its first argument
K(a,0)
or its second
K(0,b)
. Rearranging (
2.102
) we get,

K(a,0)\mathcal{K}(a)^{-1}=K(0,b)\mathcal{K}(b)=\mathfrak{K},

(2.103)

which are equal to a constant
\mathfrak{K}
since the left hand side is independent of
b
and the right hand side is independent of
a
. We can then rewrite (
2.102
) as,

K(a,b)=\mathfrak{K}\frac{\mathcal{K}(a)}{\mathcal{K}(b)}.

(2.104)

Substituting this expression back into (
2.92
) and (
2.93
) we find,

\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c)=\frac{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}{\mathcal{K}(b)}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c)=\mathfrak{K}\frac{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}{\mathcal{K}(c)}.

(2.105)

Then, using the definition
f_{\vee}(b,c)=\mathfrak{bc}
, and (
2.105
) we find,

\displaystyle df_{\vee}(b,c)

\displaystyle=\partial_{b}f_{\vee}(b,c)db+\partial_{c}f_{\vee}(b,c)dc

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}{\mathcal{K}(b)}db+\mathfrak{K}\frac{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}{\mathcal{K}(c)}dc,

(2.106)

which is rearranged to give,

\frac{df_{\vee}(b,c)}{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}=\frac{db}{\mathcal{K}(b)}+\mathfrak{K}\frac{dc}{\mathcal{K}(c)}.

(2.107)

Using (
2.101
) we can define the integral,

\phi(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{a}da^{\prime}\,\mathcal{K}(a^{\prime})^{-1}=\int_{0}^{a}da^{\prime}\,\exp\left[\int_{0}^{a^{\prime}}da^{\prime\prime}\,k(a^{\prime\prime})\right],

(2.108)

which is necessarily positive. Then, the solution to (
2.107
) is,

\phi(f_{\vee}(b,c))=\phi(b)+\mathfrak{K}\phi(c)+\alpha,

(2.109)

where
\alpha
is an integration constant. Since
\vee
is commutative, i.e.
f_{\vee}(b,c)=f_{\vee}(c,b)
, the constant
\mathfrak{K}=1
. We then have the solution for
f_{\vee}(b,c)
,

f_{\vee}(b,c)=\phi^{-1}\left(\frac{}{}\phi(b)+\phi(c)+\alpha\right).

(2.110)

In terms of the degrees of belief
\xi(a|\Gamma)
, the above becomes,

\phi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma))=\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))+\phi(\xi(b|\Gamma))+\alpha.

(2.111)

We can always regraduate our degrees of belief so that
\xi(a|\Gamma)\rightarrow\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)=\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))+\alpha
. Then the mutually exclusive sum rule becomes,

\xi^{\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)+\xi^{\prime}(b|\Gamma),

(2.112)

which is the familiar result from Section
1.3
. Application of the identity
a\vee\mathbf{F}=a
suggests that
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{F}}=0
, so that,

\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=-\alpha.

(2.113)

The distributivity constraint —

To determine the product rule, we can make similar arguments concerning derivatives. The approach in
[
25
]
makes use of the distributivity constraint (
A.21
), while in
[
27
]
we again only need associativity. The argument from
[
25
]
is as follows. Consider the distributivity condition for
\wedge
and
\vee
,

\xi(a\wedge(b\vee c)|\Gamma)=\xi\left((a\wedge b|\Gamma)\vee(a\wedge c|\Gamma)\right).

(2.114)

Using the definition of
f_{\wedge}
and
f_{\vee}
when
b
and
c
are mutually exclusive we have,

\displaystyle f_{\wedge}\left(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b\vee c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\wedge}\left(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\right)

\displaystyle+f_{\wedge}\left(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)\right).

(2.115)

We can simplify the above by identifying,

\xi(a|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a,\quad\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}b,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}c,

(2.116)

so that,
\xi(b\vee c|\Gamma\wedge a)=b+c
. Substituting these definitions into (
2.115
) gives,

f_{\wedge}(a,b+c)=f_{\wedge}(a,b)+f_{\wedge}(a,c).

(2.117)

Since the functions on the right hand side are independent of
c
and
b
resepectively, differentiating with respect to
b
and
c
we find,

\frac{\partial^{2}f_{\wedge}(a,b+c)}{\partial b\partial c}=0.

(2.118)

Likewise, letting
b+c=d
, we find,

\frac{\partial^{2}f_{\wedge}(a,d)}{\partial d^{2}}=0,

(2.119)

which shows that
f_{\wedge}
is linear in its second argument. The above is easily integrated to find,

f_{\wedge}(a,b)=f_{1}(a)b+f_{2}(a).

(2.120)

Using this solution in (
2.117
) shows that the term
f_{2}(a)=0
, thus we have,

f_{\wedge}(a,b)=f_{1}(a)b.

(2.121)

Finally, consider
\xi(a|d)=\xi(a\wedge d|d)
to evaluate,

\xi(a|d)=\xi(a\wedge d|d)=f_{\wedge}(\xi(a|d),\xi(d|a\wedge d))=f_{\wedge}(\xi(a|d),\xi_{\mathbf{T}}),

(2.122)

which according to (
2.121
) is,

\xi(a|d)=f_{1}(a|d)\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(2.123)

We can then just regraduate the function
f_{1}(a|d)
by dividing
\xi(a|d)
by
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
so that,

\frac{\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}=\frac{\xi(a|\Gamma)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\frac{\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)}{\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}.

(2.124)

The associativity constraint revisited —

The derivation of the conjunction relation in
[
27
]
uses the associativity constraint instead of distributivity,

f_{\wedge}(a,f_{\wedge}(b,c))=f_{\wedge}(f_{\wedge}(a,b),c).

(2.125)

In fact, the derivation is exactly the same as for the disjunction, up through step (
2.107
),

\frac{df_{\wedge}(b,c)}{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{bc})}=\frac{db}{\mathcal{K}(b)}+\mathfrak{K}\frac{dc}{\mathcal{K}(c)}.

(2.126)

The difference is in the definition of the solution. Define the function,

\psi(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}e^{\phi(a)}=\exp\left[\int_{0}^{a}da^{\prime}\,\mathcal{K}(a^{\prime})^{-1}\right].

(2.127)

Then, the solution to (
2.126
) is,

\alpha\psi(f_{\wedge}(a,b))=\psi(a)\psi(b)^{\mathfrak{K}}.

(2.128)

Inserting this into (
2.125
) demonstrates that again
\mathfrak{K}=1
,

\alpha\psi(f_{\wedge}(\xi(a|\Gamma),\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)))=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)).

(2.129)

To determine
\alpha
, we appeal to the special case of
\xi(a\wedge a|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)
so that (
2.129
) becomes,

\displaystyle\alpha\psi(\xi(a\wedge a|\Gamma))

\displaystyle=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma\wedge a))

\displaystyle=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}}),

(2.130)

so that,

\alpha=\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}}),

(2.131)

and therefore,

\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})\psi(\xi(a\wedge b|\Gamma))=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)).

(2.132)

One can then choose a regraduation so that
\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))/\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})
, which then leads to the recognizable form,

\xi^{\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)\xi^{\prime}(b|\Gamma\wedge a).

(2.133)

One may recognize that the distinction between the solutions (
2.110
) and (
2.129
) only differs by application of the exponential in (
2.127
), which is monotonic and hence only applies a rescaling. Nowhere in either derivation did we distinguish any defining characteristics of
\wedge
and
\vee
. Therefore, it seems that we cheated in picking (
2.108
) over (
2.127
) in the derivation of
f_{\vee}(a,b)
by peeking ahead at the answer we wanted, when the other solution is just as good. The key is to understand that one cannot use the same arguments for independently deriving the functional form of
f_{\vee}
and
f_{\wedge}
, since they are linked through the negation
f_{\neg}
. If we had instead chose (
2.127
) for the solution of
f_{\vee}(a,b)
, we would have arrived at the following,

\alpha\psi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma))=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi(b|\Gamma)).

(2.134)

Then using the special case,
\xi(a\vee\mathbf{F}|\Gamma)=\xi(a|\Gamma)
, we find that instead the constant
\alpha=\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})
,

\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})\psi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma))=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))\psi(\xi(b|\Gamma)).

(2.135)

Applying the log to both sides yields,

\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})+\phi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma))=\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))+\phi(\xi(b|\Gamma)).

(2.136)

Using the result from (
2.113
), the regraduated
\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))
is defined as,

\xi^{\prime}(a|\Gamma)=\phi(\xi(a|\Gamma))-\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}}),

(2.137)

which gives the familiar result from (
2.110
). The regraduated form of (
2.135
) on the other hand is,

\frac{\psi(\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma))}{\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})}=\frac{\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))}{\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})}\frac{\psi(\xi(b|\Gamma))}{\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})}.

(2.138)

Call
\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)=\psi(\xi(a|\Gamma))/\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})
. Then we have,

\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)\xi^{\prime\prime}(b|\Gamma).

(2.139)

Going back to the form (
2.135
), if we appeal to the special case
a\vee\neg a=\mathbf{T}
, we find,

f_{\neg}(\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma))=\xi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{T}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)^{-1}

(2.140)

Using the same arguments that lead to (
2.61
), we find for the general sum rule,

\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)=\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)\xi^{\prime\prime}(b|\Gamma)}{\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)},

(2.141)

and thus the function
f_{\wedge}(a,b)
is defined as,

\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)\xi^{\prime\prime}(b|\Gamma)}{\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\vee b|\Gamma)}.

(2.142)

This gives the sum rule in terms of a product and a corresponding product rule in terms of a ratio. If one exponentiates the solution for the product rule from (
2.124
) we find,

\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime\prime}(a|\Gamma)^{\log\xi^{\prime\prime}(b|\Gamma\wedge a)},

(2.143)

which is certainly unappealing and not intuitive, however when
b=\neg a
, we recover
\xi^{\prime\prime}(a\wedge b|\Gamma)=\xi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{F}}
as expected. Since
\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=0
, the value
\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\exp[\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})]=1
. Thus in this logarithmic scaling, total disbelief is given the value
\xi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{F}}=1
. Complete certainty on the other hand corresponds to
\xi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{T}}=\psi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})=\exp[\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})]
, which has not yet been specified. Given that the image of
\phi
is now restricted to the positive reals,
\mathrm{im}(\phi)=\mathbb{R}_{+}
, one can always regraduate the solutions so that
\phi(\xi_{\mathbf{T}})=1
which sends the image to,

\mathrm{im}(\phi)=[0,1].

(2.144)

In this scale,
\mathrm{im}(\psi)=[1,e]
.

Both solution (
2.129
) and (
2.110
) are simply the result of the associativity constraint, which both
\wedge
and
\vee
satisfy. However, once one determines the form of either
\{f_{\vee},f_{\neg}\}
or
\{f_{\wedge},f_{\neg}\}
, the remaining function is constrained through the relationships in (
2.27
) and (
2.28
) as well as with the definitions of the lower and upper bounds given from (
A.17
) and (
A.16
).

Remarks on the results —

We have identified that the rules for manipulating degrees of belief are the rules for probabilities. Hence, we make the identification
\xi\rightarrow P
and
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rightarrow P_{\mathbf{T}}=1
so that the sum and product rules for all
a|\Gamma,b|\Gamma\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
are written in the standard notation,

\displaystyle P(a\vee b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=P(a|\Gamma)+P(b|\Gamma)-P(ab|\Gamma)

(2.145)

\displaystyle P(ab|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=P(a)P(b|\Gamma a)=P(b)P(a|\Gamma b),

(2.146)

where for ease of notation we have adopted that conjunction is written as a juxtaposition,
a\wedge b\rightarrow ab
. The negation is written,

P(\neg a|\Gamma)=1-P(a|\Gamma).

(2.147)

The space of probabilities is written
\Xi\rightarrow\mathbf{P}
, which is the set of maps from
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
to the interval
[0,1]
which are consistent with the properties of
f_{\vee},f_{\wedge}
and
f_{\neg}
. Our inductive inference framework now consists of the web of beliefs
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
constrained according to the rationale
\mathcal{R}
which contains the representations of
f_{\neg},f_{\wedge}
and
f_{\vee}
.

Several authors have argued that the Cox derivation given above cannot apply generally to all universes of discourse. Specifically, Halpern
[
39
]
claims that it fails for sets
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
which are finite. His argument is that, for special cases in which the space
\Xi
is discrete, one cannot assume that
\Xi=[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an interval. Thus, the results from Cox and more importantly from Aczél do not hold. This is certainly true since the space
\Xi
must be continuous according to the equivalence in theorem (
2.1
) to hold, however there is no requirement that discrete spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
must be represented by discrete degrees of belief
\Xi
. Assuming that
\Xi
is continuous does not preclude it from representing degrees of belief for discrete spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. In other words, continuity in
\Xi
does not require that
\Xi(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
be surjective in
\Xi
.

Another argument, that the results of Cox do not have universal applicability, comes from Colyvan
[
40
]
. The author correctly points out the subtleties of calling Cox’s results an extension of propositional logic, specifically the fact that some statements of interest are not propositions at all, like the situation of incomplete arguments from chapter one. He also suggests that Cox’s results cannot be general, since they rely on preservation of the law of excluded middle, which some logics abandon. Neither of these objections are problems for us since we have already dealt with the problem of incomplete information in the definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. Colyvan’s objection only goes to show the uselessness of the intuitionist program, since the rules of probability are already known to work in the real world.

Consistency between
f_{\vee}
,
f_{\wedge}
and
f_{\neg}
—

It is easy to check that the relationships between the connectives
\vee
,
\wedge
and
\neg
are satisfied by the regraduated solutions (
2.145
) and (
2.146
).

\displaystyle P(a\vee b)

\displaystyle=P(\neg(\neg a\neg b))

\displaystyle=1-P(\neg a\neg b)=1-P(\neg a)P(\neg b|\neg a)

\displaystyle=1-[P(\neg a)P(\neg b|\neg a)+P(a)P(\neg b|a)]+P(a)P(\neg b|a)

\displaystyle=1-P(\neg b)+P(a)(1-P(b|a))

\displaystyle=P(a)+P(b)-P(a)P(b|a).

(2.148)

Likewise we have,

\displaystyle P(ab)

\displaystyle=P(\neg(\neg a\vee\neg b))

\displaystyle=1-P(\neg a\vee\neg b)=1-[P(\neg a)+P(\neg b)-P(\neg a\neg b)]

\displaystyle=1-P(\neg a)-P(\neg b)+[P(\neg a)P(\neg b|\neg a)+P(a)P(\neg b|a)]-P(a)P(\neg b|a)

\displaystyle=P(a)(1-P(\neg b|a))=P(ab).

(2.149)

From here one can form the degree of belief for any
n
-ary proposition from the fact that
\wedge
,
\vee
and
\neg
form a functionally complete set.

Modal degrees of belief? —

Now that we’ve determined a set of representations for the connectives
\Omega
, can we do the same for the modal operators? Essentially we would like to determine if some representation of the form
f_{\square}
and
f_{\diamondsuit}
can make sense in our system. The existence of such functions must be compatible with the other representations
\{f_{\Omega}\}
and must obey the simple axioms,

\displaystyle P(\square a)=f_{\square}(P(a))

\displaystyle=f_{\neg}(f_{\diamondsuit}(f_{\neg}(P(a))))

\displaystyle=1-f_{\diamondsuit}(1-P(a)),

(2.150)

and the dual,

\displaystyle P(\diamondsuit a)=f_{\diamondsuit}(P(a))

\displaystyle=f_{\neg}(f_{\square}(f_{\neg}(P(a))))

\displaystyle=1-f_{\square}(1-P(a)).

(2.151)

Depending on the axioms that one includes in
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
, one could potentially constrain the functional form of
f_{\square}
and
f_{\diamondsuit}
further. For example, if one adopts axioms
\mathbf{NC}
and
\mathbf{CN}
so that
\square(a\wedge b)\Leftrightarrow(\square a)\wedge(\square b)
, then the form of
f_{\square}
is constrained to obey,

\displaystyle P(\square(a\wedge b))

\displaystyle=f_{\square}(P(a\wedge b))

\displaystyle=f_{\square}(P(a))f_{\square}(P(b|\square a))

(2.152)

We will not pursue these ideas any further in this thesis and instead suggest that it be considered for future work.

Degrees of beliefs for predicates? —

An obvious question at this point is wether we can assign a meaningful degree of belief to some predicate
A(x)
. Certainly whenever
x
is bound by quantification the term
A(x)
just becomes a standard statement, e.g. something like
P(\exists xA(x))
is well defined. But what about the statement
P(A(x))
where
x
remains free? Such a degree of belief is not well defined, and hence the notation
P(A(x))
is not meaningful. What we can do however, is adopt a different notation for predicates which agrees with the standard meaning in probability.

Whenever an argument is a predicate in which at least one of the variables remains free, we write a lowercase
p
, so that
p(A(x))
refers to a
probability distribution function
over the predicate
A(x)
. Often times we take a shorthand notation and simply write the distribution as
p(x)
, leaving out the fact that
x
refers to a predicate
A(x)
. It is often the case that this notation can obscure meaning. When we write something like
p(x)
, we are not really writing a probability but are instead referring to a probability valued function over the variable
x
27
27

27

The most common example of this situation is whenever
x
is some subset of
\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
x\in X\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. For example,
x
could refer to the position of a particle in one dimension. Then, the predicate
A(x)
is identified as
A(x)=
“the particle is at position
x
.” Because the universe of discourse is typically mentioned ahead of time, there is seldom any confusion when we write
p(x)
to refer to the predicate
p(A(x))
.
.

Whenever a value of
x
is specified, so that
x=x_{0}
, we identify,

p(x=x_{0})=P(x_{0})=P(A(x_{0})).

(2.153)

When
x
is continuous, then the function
p(x)
is called a
probability density function
, indeed because it transforms as a density. The probability associated with some continuous interval
\Delta x\subset X
is given by the integral,

P(\Delta x)=\int_{\Delta x}dx\,p(x),

(2.154)

where
p(x)dx
is the probability assigned to the infinitesimal volume
dx
. This is nothing more than a sum of all the probabilities
P(a)
where
a\in\Delta x
. For predicates of several variables, we write the free variables separated by a comma so that
p(A(x,y))=p(x,y)
. Since the sum and product rule are valid for any statement, they can be written in terms of distributions,

\displaystyle p(x,y)

\displaystyle=p(x)p(y|x)=p(y)p(x|y),

(2.155)

\displaystyle p(x\vee y)

\displaystyle=p(x)+p(y)-p(x,y).

(2.156)

2.10
Inductive inference rules
\mathcal{Z}

We will briefly survey some important inference rules. Inductive inference rules can either be direct consequences of the axioms, or exist as rules of thumb. We will mainly focus on the former case and leave the latter to another chapter.

Bayes theorem —

One of the most important results of probability theory is that of Bayes’ theorem
[
41
]
, which was arguably first identified by Laplace. Consider the commutativity of
\wedge
so that the product rule can be written in two equivalent ways,

p(ab|\Gamma)=p(a|\Gamma)p(b|\Gamma a)=p(b|\Gamma)p(a|\Gamma b).

(2.157)

Thus, it must be the case that,

p(a|\Gamma b)=\frac{p(a|\Gamma)p(b|\Gamma a)}{p(b|\Gamma)}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad p(b|\Gamma a)=\frac{p(b|\Gamma)p(a|\Gamma b)}{p(a|\Gamma)}.

(2.158)

This is an incredibly powerful statement, for several reasons. First, this demonstrates that the web of beliefs
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
is not just constrained to be consistent with
\wedge,\vee
and
\neg
, but that once one determines
p(a|\Gamma)
and
p(b|\Gamma)
, the conditional degrees of belief
p(a|\Gamma b)
and
p(b|\Gamma a)
cannot be assigned independently. Because of this, one is able to make inferences about statements
a|\Gamma b
when
b|\Gamma a
is known and vice versa.

One can write the conditional
p(a|\Gamma b)
as a function
f_{\phi}
of the other degrees of belief,

p(a|\Gamma b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}f_{\phi}\left(\frac{}{}p(a|\Gamma),p(b|\Gamma),p(b|\Gamma a)\right),

(2.159)

or equivalently in terms of the conjunction,

p(a|\Gamma b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}f_{\phi}^{\prime}\left(\frac{}{}p(ab|\Gamma),p(a|\Gamma)\right),

(2.160)

so that either
f_{\phi}
or
f_{\phi}^{\prime}
represent the application of Bayes theorem.

Marginalization —

Sometimes one would like to conduct a joint inference on
a|\Gamma
and
b|\Gamma
, when not much is known about one of the statements. Consider that we have the joint distribution
p(ab|\Gamma)
, but we have little information about
b|\Gamma
. A convenient procedure then, is to
marginalize
over
b|\Gamma
by considering instead the probability
p(a[b\vee\neg b]|\Gamma)
,

\displaystyle p(a|\Gamma)=p(a[b\vee\neg b]|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=p(ab|\Gamma)+p(a\neg b|\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b}p(a|\Gamma)p(b|\Gamma a).

(2.161)

The distribution
p(a|\Gamma)
is called the
marginal
and equation (
2.161
) is often called the
law of total probability
. When the universe of discourse
X\times Y
is continuous, the marginal is an integral,

p(x)=\int dy\,p(x,y)=\int dy\,p(y)p(x|y).

(2.162)

Expected values —

An extremely useful concept is that of the
expected value
. For a discrete universe of discourse
A
, it is simply the weighted average over all
a\in A
,

\langle a\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{a\in A}aP(a).

(2.163)

The expected value is sometimes written
\mathbb{E}[a]=\langle a\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mu_{a}
, which is also called the
mean
. Typically when we use the words
expected value
we are referring to the weighted average over each element of the subject matter, however the words expected value can apply more generally for any power of
a
,

\langle a^{n}\rangle=\sum_{a\in X}a^{n}P(a).

(2.164)

More general still, one can define the expected value for any function of
a
,

\langle f(a)\rangle=\sum_{a\in A}f(a)P(a).

(2.165)

For continuous universes of discourse
X
the sum gets replaced by an integral,

\langle f(x)\rangle=\int_{X}dx\,f(x)p(x).

(2.166)

Bayes’ rule —

Everything we have discussed so far represents a calculus for manipulating beliefs when no new information is present. Now we ask the question of how to
update
our beliefs once new information becomes available in the form of data. We can formulate this problem in terms of deciding how one should assign a new web of beliefs
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
given some new information about
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. We will represent a generic change in the web of beliefs by some function
\star:\mathbf{Q}\rightarrow\mathbf{P}
so that
\mathbf{Q}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{\star}\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
.

Consider that we have some prior information about the relationship between two statements
a|\Gamma
and
b|\Gamma
, whose joint distribution is given by,

q(ab|\Gamma)=q(a|\Gamma)q(b|\Gamma a)\in\mathbf{Q},

(2.167)

which we call the
prior
. Given new information about
b|\Gamma
, we would like to determine a suitable
posterior

p(a|\Gamma)\in\mathbf{P}
. It is important to emphasize at this point that the universe of discourse does not consist of just the statements
a|\Gamma
or
b|\Gamma
, but of both. This will be important later when we construct a generalization to Bayes’ rule.

Consider now that we have collected new information about
b|\Gamma
, such that the value of
b|\Gamma
is known, i.e.
b|\Gamma\xrightarrow{\star}b^{\prime}|\Gamma
so that the posterior web of beliefs is constrained to satisfy,

q(b|\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\star}p(b|\Gamma)=\sum_{a}p(ab|\Gamma)=\delta_{bb^{\prime}}.

(2.168)

This is not enough to constrain the form of
p(a|\Gamma)\in\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
, since many posteriors will satisfy this rule and be internally consistent with respect to the rationale. In order to constrain the form of
p(a|\Gamma)
, one needs to invoke some new principle
[
25
]
inspired by parsimony,

Principle 2.5

(Principle of Minimal Updating)
.

The web of beliefs
\mathbf{Q}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
should only be revised to the extent required by the new information.

Like we have said, such a constraint is not necessary for an updating scheme to be consistent, but is merely imposed to satisfy some ethical desire for how we
should
conduct inference
[
25
]
. In the case of the joint posterior distribution,

p(ab|\Gamma)=p(a|\Gamma)p(b|\Gamma a)=p(a|\Gamma b)\delta_{bb^{\prime}},

(2.169)

the conditional
p(a|\Gamma b)
is at this point still arbitrary. If we invoke the principle of minimal updating (PMU), then nothing requires us to change the distribution
p(a|\Gamma b)
since the change is already invoked in
\delta_{bb^{\prime}}
. Thus we identify,

p(a|\Gamma b)=q(a|\Gamma b),

(2.170)

so that the web of posterior beliefs
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
is given by,

p(ab^{\prime}|\Gamma)=q(a|\Gamma b^{\prime})\delta_{bb^{\prime}}

(2.171)

The posterior distribution
p(a|\Gamma)
is then given by marginalizing over
b|\Gamma
,

p(a|\Gamma)=\sum_{b}q(a|\Gamma b^{\prime})\delta_{bb^{\prime}}=q(a|\Gamma b^{\prime}).

(2.172)

This identifies the updated posterior distribution
p(a|\Gamma)
as the
prior conditional distribution

q(a|\Gamma b^{\prime})
, which is called
Bayes’ rule
and is often written as,

q(a|\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\star}p(a|\Gamma)=q(a|\Gamma)\frac{q(b^{\prime}|\Gamma a)}{q(b^{\prime}|\Gamma)}.

(2.173)

Typically the conditional distribution
q(b^{\prime}|\Gamma a)
is called the
likelihood
and the factor,

q(b^{\prime}|\Gamma)=\sum_{a}q(ab^{\prime}|\Gamma),

(2.174)

is called the
evidence
. Some may point out that (
2.173
) is simply the same thing as Bayes’ theorem in (
2.158
), however the theorem refers only to a consequence of the product rule in
\mathbf{Q}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
. The action of
assigning
to the degree of belief
p(a|\Gamma)
the conditional
q(a|\Gamma b)
is something entirely different since we changing the web
\mathbf{Q}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{\star}\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
.

2.11
Other approaches

There exist a plethora of other approaches for constructing the rules of probability in the literature. We have mentioned several already that have been inspired by Cox, such as Caticha
[
25
,
42
,
43
]
, Fine
[
44
]
, Jaynes
[
26
,
45
]
, Knuth
[
35
,
36
]
, Paris
[
24
]
, Skilling
[
28
]
, Smith and Erickson
[
46
]
, Tribus
[
47
]
, Van Horn
[
48
,
49
]
and Vanslette
[
31
]
. We will briefly discuss some of the other popular approaches, such as the
subjective
and
frequentist
approaches and their similarities, as well as their differences, with the approach in this thesis.

De Finetti —

My thesis, paradoxically, and a little provocatively, but nonetheless genuinely, is simply this: PROBABILITY DOES NOT EXIST. The abandonment of superstitious beliefs about the existence of Phlogiston, the Cosmic Ether, Absolute Space and Time,…, or Fairies and Witches, was an essential step along the road to scientific thinking. Probability, too, if regarded as something endowed with some kind of objective existence, is no less a misleading misconception, an illusory attempt to exteriorize or materialize our true probabilistic beliefs.

Bruno de Finetti
, 1970

Bruno de Finetti championed the idea that probabilities are inherently subjective and do not
exist
in any objective sense. This idea is the overarching theme of what is called the
subjective approach
to probability theory
28
28

28

Perhaps the main difficulty with de Finetti’s approach is the confusion over the dichotomies
subjective vs. objective
and
ontic vs. epistemic
. The problem is that often times when one says “subjective” (personal) what they really mean is “epistemic” (having to do with knowledge). While we take the view that probabilities are inherently epistemic, they can still be either subjective or objective depending on the subject matter. For example, as we will see in Chapters six, seven and eight, in quantum mechanics probabilities play a central role and are entirely epistemic quantities, however they are not subjective. The probabilities which appear in QM are objective in the sense that they are constrained to agree with the results of experiment.
. De Finetti’s idea was that probabilities are assignments of personal belief which represent how one would
bet
on a particular outcome of some event given their current information. In this way, probabilities are intimately tied to
actions
, which is quite different from what we have developed in this chapter. In our treatment, the probability calculus determines what one ought to
believe
, but does not suggest any method for determining what one ought to
do
with those beliefs. Those ideas belong to a different subject altogether, such as
decision theory
or
game theory
.

De Finetti’s contemporaries, Ramsey
[
50
]
and Savage
[
51
]
, developed a similar approach in which the specific scheme of betting with money was replaced with a more general concept of
utility
. This idea later culminated in utility theories developed by von Neumann and Morgenstern
[
52
]
. In this scheme, one is required to specify a
utility function

u:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
X
is the subject matter. Depending on the treatment, the utility function is constrained to obey a set of axioms
[
52
]
. The central quantity of interest is the expected utility,

\langle u(x)\rangle=\sum_{x\in X}u(x)p(x).

(2.175)

As was shown by von Neumann and Morgenstern
[
52
]
, by assuming that the function
u(x)
obeys certain properties, such as transitivity and continuity, then a rational agent will make decisions which maximize (
2.175
).

The problem with utilitarian approaches is that they single out universes of discourse which only apply to the behavior of the rational agent. Utilitarianism is certainly useful for the stock market and even for the design of physical experiments, but it is not useful for physics as a whole. Even so, the idea that probabilities are inherently subjective was an important step for bringing inference into the 20th century. One use of the de Finetti approach in physics is
quantum Bayesianism
, or
QBism

[
53
]
.

Kolmogorov —

Perhaps the most popular approach to probability theory is the one developed by Kolmogorov
[
54
]
in which probabilities are
measures
associated to some set
X
. The formalism was developed as a means for assigning probabilities, or measures, to volumes in spaces such as
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A measure is a function
\mu
from a
\sigma
-algebra over
X
to the extended real numbers
\mathbb{R}\cup[-\infty,\infty]
. A
\sigma
-algebra for
X
is a subset of the power set
\Sigma\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)
which has the following properties,

Axioms 2.1

(
\sigma
-algebra)
.

Let
X
be a set. A collection of subsets
\Sigma\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)
is called a
\sigma
-algebra if,

1.

The entire set
X
and the empty set
\emptyset
are in
\Sigma
,

X,\emptyset\in\Sigma.

(2.176)

2.

\Sigma
is closed under complementation,

\forall A\in\Sigma:X\backslash A\in\Sigma.

(2.177)

3.

\Sigma
is closed under countable unions.

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\Sigma:\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}A_{k}\in\Sigma.

(2.178)

Due to De Morgans laws axioms (
2.177
) and (
2.178
) are equivalent to,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\Sigma:\bigcap_{k=1}^{n}A_{k}\in\Sigma.

(2.179)

These axioms demonstrate that a
\sigma
-algebra is essentially an algebra of sets with the additional requirement that countable unions be in
\Sigma
. In special cases,
\sigma
-algebras
(\Sigma,\cup,\cap,\backslash)
are isomorphic to the Boolean algebra
(\mathcal{A},\vee,\wedge,\neg)
whenever
\mathcal{A}
is finite
29
29

29

There are some subtleties with the differences between
\sigma
-algebras and Boolean algebras. Due to Stone’s representation theorem, Boolean algebras are isomorphic to some field of sets, which may only require closure under
finite
unions, i.e. given a finite sequence of subsets
A_{i},\dots,A_{n}
of the algebra
X
, we have
\forall n<\infty:\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\in X
. Any
\sigma
-algebra however, is
completed
with respect to the union, so that it is closed under a countable number of unions (axiom (
2.178
)). The reason for axiom (
2.178
) actually comes from a different direction that is related to the Banach-Tarski paradox
[
55
]
. In order to assign a meaningful volume to
n
-dimensional spaces, Banach and Tarski showed that one of four assumptions must be true, either

1.

The volume of a set could change under rotation.

2.

One must abandon the axiom of choice.

3.

The volume of the union of two disjoint sets is not necessarily equal to the sum of their volumes.

4.

There are some sets which are
non-measureable
.

Obviously solutions (1) and (3) are undesirable. The opted for solution has been number (4), for which we need to develop the notion of a
measurable set
. Axiom (
2.178
) is explicitly included to avoid the problems of the Banach-Tarski paradox.
. If the Boolean algebra
(\mathcal{A},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
is complete and the set
\mathcal{A}
is countable, then the properties of
(\mathcal{A},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
are
stronger
than the axioms in
1.1
. In this sense, a
\sigma
-algebra is a sort of in-between for these two situations.

A space
X
together with a sigma algebra
\Sigma
is called a
measurable space

\langle X,\Sigma\rangle
. A measure
\mu
on
\Sigma
is then defined as,

Axioms 2.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a
\sigma
-algebra over
X
. A function
\mu:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup[-\infty,\infty]
is called a measure if,

1.

The function
\mu
is non-negative,

\forall A\in\Sigma:\mu(A)\geq 0.

(2.180)

2.

The empty set has zero measure,

\mu(\emptyset)=0.

(2.181)

3.

For all countable collections
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
, the function
\mu
is countably additive over disjoint subsets,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}:\forall k\neq\ell A_{k}\cap A_{\ell}=\emptyset:\mu\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}A_{k}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu(A_{k}).

(2.182)

Finally, whenever
\mu(X)=1
we say that
\mu
is a
probability measure
over
\Sigma
. While the axioms for
\sigma
-algebras and measures are reasonable, they have the problem of lacking an interpretation. The set
X
could be anything, such as an interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
. By assigning a probability measure to subsets of
[c,d]\subset[a,b]
, what exactly is the meaning of
\mu([c,d])
? What is
\mu([c,d])
the probability of? By lacking an interpretation, this approach can lead to confusion.

There is also the additional problem of not having a definition of a conditional probability a priori. It has to be put in by hand after the fact by defining,

\mu(a|b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\mu(ab)}{\mu(b)}.

(2.183)

What’s worse about this situation is that the Kolmogorov approach is ill-equipped to handle an updating procedure. Because there is no interpretation as to what the measure
\mu(a|b)
is supposed to represent, the procedure in
Section 1.6
is not well defined.

In our approach we fix the problem of the interpretation from the beginning. The subject matter is not some generic set
X
, but the set of statements
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. Some may argue that this is less
general
than Kolmogorov because we do not allow the subject matter to be arbitrary sets
X
, but so be it. The problem with Kolmogorov is that it is
too
general and completely lacks an interpretation, which has the potential to allow fallacious reasoning.

Frequentism —

In the long run we shall all be dead.

Keynes
, 1923

There are many “interpretations” of probability theory which claim that probabilities are frequencies, or
expectations of chance
given some large ensemble of repeated events. The appeal of this interpretation is that one can determine frequencies of random events by simply counting a number of positive occurrences in the ensemble. Such an assignment of probability is independent of any agent, be they ideally rational or not, and hence one feels that they have constructed some objective notion of probability.

There are several philosophical and practical objections to this idea. The biggest problem is that frequentism relies on there being a large number of repeated trials, or that the situation in question be repeatable in principle. Thus, inferences about which there are no such ensembles cannot be defined in the frequentist interpretation. A question such as, “what is the probability that there is life on Mars?” is not even a legitimate question, since there is only one Mars and not an ensemble. To combat these issues a frequentist may simply agree, that such a question is nonsensical, and that probabilities are
only
defined in the presence of repeatable events. Such an admission greatly reduces the power of any inference framework, which is undesirable. On the contrary, they may attempt to justify such questions on the basis of the possible worlds semantics so that one constructs a
virtual ensemble
, but this destroys any notion of objectivity that one may have gained from the interpretation in the first place.

The frequentist interpretation also tends to be rather vague, despite its claim of being objective. One source of vagueness is the meaning of the word
random
. The popular choice for defining random variables is to define a
probability space

P
, which is a triple
P=\{\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu\}
, where
\Omega
is a set of mutually exclusive and exhaustive outcomes and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
is a collection of subsets of
\Omega
called the
event space
. The event space
\mathcal{F}
is a
\sigma
-algebra
generated by
\Omega
and hence is equivalent to a Boolean algebra in special cases. One then defines a random variable
X:(\Omega,\Sigma)\rightarrow(E,T)
as a
measureable function
30
30

30

A measureable function
f:X\rightarrow Y
between two measurable spaces
(X,\Sigma)
and
(Y,T)
preserves the structure of the underlying
\sigma
-algebras, i.e. the preimage of any
B\in T
is in
\Sigma
.
from the measurable space
(\Omega,\Sigma)
to some other measureable space
(E,T)
. Then, the measure associated to whether the function
X
assigns values to some subset
S\subseteq E
in the target is defined by,

\mu\left(X\in S\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mu\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega\,\middle|\,X(\omega)\in S\right\}\right).

(2.184)

In this sense,
X
is just a function which groups the elements of
\Omega
into particular subsets. An analogous construction in
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
would be a homomorphism
\mathcal{X}:\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
to another Boolean algebra
(\tilde{\mathcal{B}},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
such that for every element
b\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, the preimage
\mathcal{X}^{-1}(b)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. While all of this is perfectly well defined, it has already obscured the subject matter enough to make the effort seem not worthwhile.

One of the biggest proponents of the frequentist view was Fisher
[
56
]
, who originally started out as a Bayesian but was unable to deal with the problem of the assignment of the prior
[
57
]
. Fisher then became obsessed with trying to remove all subjectivity from inductive inference, which is practically impossible. No matter what one does, there will always be some subjective component left over in any inference where one has incomplete information. Savage refers to Fisher’s attempt as “a bold attempt to make the Bayesian omelet without breaking the Bayesian egg”
[
58
]
.

Quantum probabilities? —

There have been attempts at arguing that classical inductive inference is incomplete, since quantum theory seems to demonstrate a violation of the sum and product rules. As was shown in
[
25
]
, these arguments are simply a misunderstanding of the problem. We will demonstrate the results of the argument here.

The prototypical example for why classical probability theory is incompatible with quantum mechanics will make use of the superposition principle and the role of interference effects. Consider the gedanken two slit experiment in which a particle
q
is prepared at a source
s
with some velocity in the direction of two slits
a
and
b
. The particle then has some probability of going through either slit
a
or slit
b
and then landing somewhere on the screen on the other side at location
x
,

Figure 8:
A two slit experiment where a particle is prepared at source
s
and then passes through either slit
a
or slit
b
and arrives at some location on the screen.

In quantum mechanics, the states that describe the particle going through slit
a
and
b
are represented by the wave functions,
\psi_{a}
and
\psi_{b}
respectively. The state that describes the particle arriving at
x
is then given by the superposition
\psi_{ab}\propto\psi_{a}+\psi_{b}
, where the probability of detection at
x
is,

p_{ab}(x)=|\psi_{ab}|^{2}\propto|\psi_{a}+\psi_{b}|^{2}=|\psi_{a}|^{2}+|\psi_{b}|^{2}+2\mathrm{Re}[\psi_{a}^{*}\psi_{b}].

(2.185)

The term
|\psi_{a}|^{2}
corresponds to the probability of detection at
x
when the particle passes through slit
a

and
when slit
b
is closed. Likewise
|\psi_{b}|^{2}
is the probability that the particle passes through slit
b
when slit
a
is closed. Since the particle must pass through either slit
a
or slit
b
, it looks like quantum mechanics violates the sum rule since for these mutually exclusive events we find,

p_{ab}(x)\neq|\psi_{a}|^{2}+|\psi_{b}|^{2}.

(2.186)

The problem, which was originally suggested by Feynman
[
59
]
, is actually more subtle. A counter argument was given by Koopman
[
60
]
, as well as in Ballentine
[
61
]
and Caticha
[
25
]
. Following Caticha, consider first that we enumerate the various statements in the problem as follows,

•

s=
“particle
q
is generated at the source
s
.

•

a=
“slit
a
is open” and
\neg a=
“slit
a
is closed.”

•

b=
“slit
b
is open” and
\neg b=
“slit
b
is closed.”

•

\alpha=
“particle
q
goes through slit
a
.”

•

\beta=
“particle
q
goes through slit
b
.”

•

x=
“particle
q
is detected at
x
.”

The statement of interest is then,
x|sab
, or,

“The particle
q
is detected at
x
, given that it was generated at source
s
and slit
a
and slit
b
are both open.”

A conjunction of interest will be
[\alpha\vee\beta]x|sab
, whose probability can be determined as,

p([\alpha\vee\beta]x|sab)=p(\alpha x|sab)+p(\beta x|sab)-p(\alpha\beta x|sab).

(2.187)

Since the particle cannot go through both slits at the same time, the conjunction vanishes,

p(\alpha\beta x|sab)=0.

(2.188)

Thus we have,

p([\alpha\vee\beta]x|sab)=p(\alpha x|sab)+p(\beta x|sab).

(2.189)

Likewise, we can also write
p([\alpha\vee\beta]x|sab)
using the product rule,

p([\alpha\vee\beta]x|sab)=p(x|sab)p([\alpha\vee\beta]|sabx).

(2.190)

However, since the particle must go through either slit
a
or
b
if it was detected at
x
, then the second term
p([\alpha\vee\beta]|sabx)=
. Thus we have,

p(x|sab)=p(\alpha x|sab)+p(\beta x|sab).

(2.191)

Finally, the corresponding equation for (
2.186
) is,

p(x|sab)\neq p(\alpha x|sa\neg b)+p(\beta x|s\neg ab).

(2.192)

But, as we have shown in (
2.191
), this discrepancy is perfectly consistent with the results of probability theory, hence there is no contradiction. However, one may be tempted to use the product rule in (
2.191
) so that we have,

p(x|sab)=p(\alpha|sab)p(x|sab\alpha)+p(\beta|sab)p(sab\beta).

(2.193)

Our classical intuition tells us that, if the particle passes through slit
a
, then the fact that slit
b
is open makes no difference and hence one can make the substitution,

p(x|sab\alpha)\rightarrow p(x|sa\neg b\alpha),

(2.194)

and likewise for the second term in (
2.193
). Thus, one would be led to the inference,

\displaystyle p(x|sab)

\displaystyle=p(\alpha|sa\neg b)p(x|sa\neg b\alpha)+p(\beta|s\neg ab)p(s\neg ab\beta)

\displaystyle=p(\alpha x|sa\neg b)+p(\beta x|s\neg ab),

(2.195)

which is incorrect. This demonstrates that our classical intuition about whether distant objects can influence quantum systems is wrong, quantum mechanics can be non-local.

2.12
Summary

In this chapter we constructed a theory of inference by designing the rules for probabilities as degrees of rational belief. By constraining representations (
Section 3.2.1
) of elements of the Boolean algebra over extended proposition space (
Section 2.6
) to adhere to certain criteria, we arrived at the sum and product rules for probabilities. We introduced the
principle of minimal updating
(
Section 3.6
) in order to derive Bayes’ rule. In the next chapter we will generalize Bayes’ rule to situations in which one wishes to update their beliefs in the presence of constraints.

3
Entropic inference

The theory of probability, if it is to be useful at all, demands a theory for updating probabilities.

Ariel Caticha
, 2020

At the end of the previous chapter, we had constructed an inductive inference for dealing with states of partial knowledge and a calculus for manipulating them in a way consistent with some rationale,
\mathcal{R}
. This answered the first of the two questions posed in the Introduction, the second of which –
how does one update their beliefs in the presence of new information
– we will address in this chapter. As the quotation above suggets, any inference framework would be remiss if it didn’t also contain a method for updating ones beliefs whenever new information becomes available.

The rationale for inductive inference was developed to express several desirable properties including
Universal Applicability
,
Consistency
,
Practicality
and
Parsimony
. We proceed in much the same way by invoking a similar rationale,
\mathcal{R}_{S}
, for the purposes of updating. We have already seen some instances of this with the
Principle of Minimal Updating
which was imposed in the derivation of Bayes’ rule at the end of chapter two. As systematic as Bayes’ rule is, it only provides a way for updating beliefs when new information comes in the form of data. This raises the central question of this chapter: How should we update our beliefs when new information does not come in the form of data? Before we can address that question, we must first clarify what is meant after all by
information
.

The tool for updating that we will construct is commonly referred to as the
Maximum Entropy method
(ME)
[
25
]
which has its roots in
MaxEnt

[
26
]
. Jaynes developed MaxEnt for the purposes of assigning priors from arbitrary constraints
[
45
,
57
]
. While this helps to identify constraints as being a
type of information
different from data, MaxEnt is ultimately not a tool for updating, hence it cannot take into account arbitrary priors
[
62
]
. On the other hand, Bayes’ rule allows for arbitrary priors but does not allow for arbitrary constraints. The goal of constructing the ME method will be to unify both Bayes’ rule and MaxEnt into a complete method for inductive inference – which we call
entropic inference
– so that one can update arbitrary priors on the basis of arbitrary constraints. This is done by generalizing the PMU.

In most circles, the words
entropy
and
information
are invoked in the context of
Information Theory

[
63
]
. Originally developed by Shannon
[
64
]
, Information Theory is mainly concerned with sending bits of information across communication channels and analysing the degrading effects of noise. Through the use of various axioms, Shannon constructed his entropy as a means of quantifying an
amount
of information that is missing from a probability distribution. While certainly useful in the context of communications theory, the Shannon entropy – which was later adopted in the context of MaxEnt – can only refer to discrete variables; for continuous variables it is undefined. One can get around this problem by introducing an invariant measure into the definition so that the entropy takes the form of the
Kullback-Leibler divergence
31
31

31

The KL divergence was actually first written down by Gibbs
[
Gibbs
]
.
(KL). Despite this, the Shannon entropy was constructed using axioms which have nothing to do with updating beliefs. As pointed out by Caticha
[
25
]
, this presents a serious problem since one could have introduced different axioms which would lead to different entropies (such as the Renyi
[
65
]
or Tsallis
[
66
]
types). Given all of the possibilities, which functional should we choose?

The revolution in approaches to using entropy – and the answer to the previous question – begins with a paper by Shore and Johnson
[
67
]
in which they derive the functional form of the relative entropy for the purposes of updating. Their approach was to define a set of axioms that constrain the updating procedure, which they then use to determine the functional form of the entropy via
eliminative induction
. This approach of axiomitizing the updating process, rather than the measure itself, gives incredible clarity to the subject of inference – while previous justifications for using entropy relied on discussing the properties of the functional, Shore and Johnson instead construct the entropy as the tool for incorporating new information. This point is made clear in the introduction to their paper,

“Most [approaches to entropy] are based on a formal description of what is required of an information measure; none are based on a formal description of what is required of a method for taking information into account.” (Shore and Johnson, 1980)

While Shore and Johnson’s approach provided a systematic way of constructing an entropy functional for the purposes of updating, they had not addressed the question of why one should use a variational principle in the first place, i.e.
why the max in MaxEnt
. The explanation for this came in Skilling’s paper “The Axioms of Maximum Entropy”
[
68
]
in which he makes explicit the desire for a tool which
ranks
posterior distributions with respect to some
order of preference
. In
[
68
]
he states
32
32

32

In his paper
[
68
]
, Skilling was mainly concerned with reconstructing images with incomplete data.
,

“We aim to provide an image [posterior distribution] which is the ‘best’ according to an agreed criterion. This involves setting up a ranking procedure which determines which of two images is ‘better’. To avoid circularity, and to ensure that there is always some image which is not ‘bettered’ by any other, we impose the transitivity requirement,
(f better than g) and (g better than h)
\Rightarrow
(f better than h).” (Skilling, 1988)

Once we specify that what we desire is a tool for ranking probabilities – which in turn is the relative entropy – then maximizing it to find the best posterior is completely natural, intuitive and no longer requires any ad hoc justification.

Another set of advancements for entropic methods of inference comes from Caticha
[
25
]
. His contributions to the subject include answering questions such as,
what constitutes information
and
are entropic and Bayesian methods compatible
, both of which we will discuss in this chapter. Despite showing that Bayes’ rule is a special case of the ME method, his work on the subject
[
69
,
Caticha_Giffin_2
]
is perhaps still largely unappreciated. Before he and his student A. Giffin published their results, it was largely believed that Bayesian and entropic methods referred to two different and unconnected (or even incompatible) ideas.

Caticha’s original paper
[
70
]
introduced the
Principle of Minimal Updating
(PMU) which we had used for constraining Bayes’ rule in the previous chapter. The idea, which is central to the Bayesian philosophy, allowed Caticha to design the relative entropy using only three design criteria, whereas previous approaches (such as Shore and Johnson, Skilling) required at least four. As we already mentioned, the design derivation in this thesis follows closely the work of Caticha by adopting the PMU and the same design criteria found in
[
25
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
]
.

Apart from answering the question of how we update from a prior to a posterior, we also wish to construct a tool for the purposes of
ranking
joint distributions with respect to their
correlations
. Correlations are a central component to any study involving inference, so the desire for quantitative measures is felt universally. Such measures are plentiful in information theory, however here our goal is to design one according to first principles. A design derivation for correlation functionals had not been attempted before K. Vanslette and myself published our results on the matter in the journal Entropy
[
74
]
. We will follow the design derivation of that paper in this thesis.

As we will see, the
type
of correlation functional
I[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})]
one arrives at depends on a choice of the
splits
within the proposition space
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, and thus the functional we seek is
I[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})]\rightarrow I[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}),\tilde{\mathcal{A}}]
. For example, if one has a proposition space
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\dots\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
, consisting of
N
variables, then one must specify
which
correlations the functional
I[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}),\tilde{\mathcal{A}}]
should quantify. Do we wish to quantify how the variable
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
is correlated with the other
N-1
variables? Or do we want to study the correlations
between
all of the variables? In our design derivation, each of these questions represent the extremal cases of the family of quantifiers
I[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}),\tilde{\mathcal{A}}]
, the former being a
bi-partite
correlation (or mutual information) functional and the latter being a
total
correlation functional.

In the main design derivation we will focus on the the case of
total correlation
which is designed to quantify the correlations between every variable subspace
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
in a set of variables
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\times\dots\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
. We suggest a set of design criteria (DC) for the purpose of designing such a tool. These DC are guided by the
Principle of Constant Correlations
(PCC), which states that “the amount of correlations in
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
should not change unless required by the transformation,
(\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}),\tilde{\mathcal{A}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow}}(\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
.” This implies our design derivation requires us to study equivalence classes of
[\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})]
within statistical manifolds
\mathbf{\Delta}
under the various transformations of distributions
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
that are typically performed in inference tasks. We will find, according to our design criteria, that the global quantifier of correlations we desire in this special case is equivalent to the total correlation
[
75
]
.

Once one arrives at the TC as the solution to the design problem, one can then form special cases such as the
mutual information

[
63
]
or, as we will call them, any
n
-partite information
NPI which measures the correlations shared between generic
n
-partitions of the proposition space. The NPI can also be derived using the same principles as the TC except with one modification, as we will discuss in
Section V
.

The special case of NPI when
n=2
is the
bipartite
(or mutual) information, which quantifies the
amount
of correlations present between two subsets of some proposition space
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. Mutual information (MI) as a measure of correlation has a long history, beginning with Shannon’s seminal work on communication theory
[
64
]
in which he first defines it. While Shannon provided arguments for the functional form of his entropy
[
64
]
, he did not provide an axiomatization of MI.

Information?

Information is what information does, it changes your mind.

Ariel Caticha
, 2020

Information can mean many different things depending on the context in which it is used. For example, the subject of
Information Theory
considers information to be something that is transmitted from a
source
to some
destination
through a
channel
and this information can be measured, i.e. we can quantify an
amount of information
. Information theory however has little to do with the beliefs of rational agents, much less with updating them. Hence, this view of information is unsatisfactory for our purposes.

We also often hear that information is “physical,” that physical systems “carry” information. This view was likely inspired by information theory in the interests of understanding the limitations of computation. Questions such as,
what is the minimum amount of energy required to store a single bit of information
, or,
how much energy is expended when we throw away information
, suggest that a piece of information must, in the very least, be represented by something physical. This idea was pushed further by Landauer who first argued it in his 1991 article “Information is physical”
[
76
]
that has inspired many others in the field. He later wrote
[
77
]
,

“Information is not an abstract entity but exists only through a physical representation, thus tying it to all the restrictions and possibilities of our real physical universe.” (Landauer, 1999)

Essentially, all information must somehow be coupled to the physical world in order to be transmitted from one agent to another, whether it be words inscribed on rock or the electromagnetic waves sent through an ethernet cable. Therefore, the distinction between some deeper understanding of what constitutes information and its physical representation become unimportant – one simply adopts the pragmatic view that information is physical itself so that no further abstraction is needed.

John Wheeler suggested a similar idea in his paper “Information, Physics, Quantum: The Search for Links,” in which he coined the term “it from bit”
[
78
]
. The idea of “it from bit” is that every constituent of existence can be described by a series of yes/no questions, such as
is the position of the particle ‘x’
. By answering either “yes” or “no” to such a question, one is associating a single classical bit of information to the description of a physical object. Consequently, every physical entity is defined by a set of unique answers of “yes” and “no.” While this topic is certainly interesting, the idea that information is physical turns out to be too restrictive for the purposes of inference.

Here we adopt the pragmatic view that information is an epistemic quantity. In this sense, information is what causes us to change our beliefs – it is what we seek when we ask a question. Indeed, since the inductive inference framework was developed on the basis of reasoning with
incomplete
information, it is only natural to seek more complete information, with the goal of improving our ability to reason. Following Caticha
[
25
]
, we state the defining characteristics of information,

“Information is what affects and therefore constraints rational beliefs. Information is what forces a change in beliefs.” (Caticha, 2020)

In this epistemic definition, there is no notion of an
amount
of information that can be measured. New information is simply a set of constraints on our beliefs and the constraints specify what we should believe given that we were ideally rational.

There is an instructive analogy of our definition of information with that of physics, which was presented in
[
25
]
. In classical mechanics, a system is defined by its
quantity of motion
, its momentum. The systems state of motion changes whenever it is acted upon by forces – an initial state
\vec{p}_{i}
changes to some final state
\vec{p}_{f}
under the influence of some external force
\vec{F}_{\mathrm{ext.}}
. In inference a state is defined by
degrees of belief
, a set of probabilities
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
. Information then, is the
force
which causes a change from some initial set of beliefs,
\mathbf{Q}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
, to a final set of beliefs,
\mathbf{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
. In this sense, updating beliefs is a form of dynamics. A generalization to this dynamics is discussed in chapter four, and the application of its generalization to physics as an
Entropic Dynamics
is discussed in part two of this thesis.

Correlations? —

The term correlation may be defined colloquially as being a relation between two or more “things".
While we have a sense of what correlations are, how do we quantify this notion more precisely? If correlations have to do with “things” in the real world, are correlations themselves “real?” Can correlations be “physical?” In the context of inference, correlations are broadly defined as being statistical relationships between propositions. In this thesis we adopt the view that whatever correlations may be, their effect is to influence our beliefs about the natural world. In this sense, correlations are just a particular type of information. More specifically,

Correlations are the pieces of information that constrain conditional beliefs
.

The design for correlation quantifiers can be motivated by first examining the more intuitive extreme cases. Perhaps the most intuitive situation occurs when two systems are actually independent – the joint system contains no correlations whatsoever. We would expect in this case that our joint beliefs would factor,
p(a,b)=p(a)p(b)
, since knowing either the value of
a
or
b
tells us nothing about the other. The other extreme happens when
a
and
b
are completely determined by each other – the joint system is maximally correlated. In this case, we would expect our joint beliefs to behave as
p(a,b)=p(a)\delta(b-f_{a}(a))=p(b)p(a-f_{b}(b))
where the delta functions signify that
b
is some function of
a
,
b=f_{a}(a)
, and that this function is invertible,
f_{a}=f_{b}^{-1}
. While we understand both of these edge cases, it is not entirely clear how one quantifies anything that happens in the middle. If we are given two joint distributions
p_{1}(a,b)=p_{1}(a)p_{1}(b|a)
and
p_{2}(a,b)=p_{2}(a)p_{2}(b|a)
