Often in applications of Boolean algebra, the disjunction is written as a plus sign,
a\vee b\rightarrow a
“
+
”
b
, conjunction is omitted,
a\wedge b\rightarrow ab
, and the equivalence connective is written as an equals sign,
a\Leftrightarrow b\rightarrow a
“
=
”
b
. We shall not use this notation however, since it leads to confusion with the algebra over fields.

If the statement
a\Leftrightarrow b
is true, then the two propositions
a
and
b
are
equivalent in their truth value
, but they are not necessarily “equal” as statements. To say that two propositions are “equal” is perhaps misleading. An example of this can best be seen in the following,

a\Leftrightarrow(a\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{T}).

(A.7)

Translated into English, this means that any statement such as “a is true,” is
equivalent
to the proposition “a.” This may seem strange, since they are different statements, but the key here is that they are only equivalent with respect to their “truth” values, thus the use of the equivalence connective
\Leftrightarrow
in (
A.7
) rather than an equals sign.

Functional completeness —

The set of connectives
\{\wedge,\vee,\neg\}
are
functionally complete

[
97
]
, which means that they act as generators for all other
n
-ary connectives on the bivalent valued propositions, i.e. they provide a
basis
for the Boolean algebra of propositions. This can be seen through the following set of definitions,

The connectives
\wedge
,
\vee
and
\neg
as generators

1.

Not and
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\uparrow b\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\wedge b)
.

2.

Not or
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\downarrow b\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\vee b)
.

3.

Implication
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\Rightarrow b\Leftrightarrow\neg a\vee b
.

4.

Exclusive or
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\dot{\vee}b\Leftrightarrow(a\vee b)\wedge\neg(a\wedge b)
.

5.

Equivalence
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:(a\Leftrightarrow b)\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\vee b)\vee(a\wedge b)
.

While the set
\{\wedge,\vee,\neg\}
can generate all other
n
-ary connectives, it is not
minimal
, since
or
can be written using
\wedge
and
\neg
, and similarly
and
can written in terms of
\vee
and
\neg
as in De Morgan’s laws from (
3
),

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee b

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg(\neg a\wedge\neg b),

(A.8)

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge b

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg(\neg a\vee\neg b).

(A.9)

This establishes the correspondence,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\neg,\neg)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\neg}

Figure 9:
Commutative diagram between,
\neg\circ\wedge\Leftrightarrow\vee\circ(\neg,\neg)
, and,
\neg\circ\vee\Leftrightarrow\wedge\circ(\neg,\neg)
.

The most minimal functionally complete connectives are the
not and
and
not or
operators. Typically,
not and
,
\uparrow
, is used in the study of digital circuits, from which the three connectives
\{\wedge,\vee,\neg\}
can be defined as follows,

The connective
\uparrow
as a minimal generator

1.

Negation
-
\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\neg a\Leftrightarrow a\uparrow a
.

2.

And
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge b\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\uparrow b)\Leftrightarrow(a\uparrow b)\uparrow(a\uparrow b)
.

3.

Or
-
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee b\Leftrightarrow(a\uparrow a)\uparrow(b\uparrow b)
.

Once one has these three generators, one can define the entire set of connectives, and hence
\uparrow
is functionally complete. A similar set of connectives holds for the
not or
connective,
\downarrow
, which one gets by replacing the
\uparrow
in the above list with
\downarrow
and
\wedge
with
\vee
. Another popular basis is the
ring basis
, which consists of the connectives
\{\wedge,\dot{\vee}\}

[
98
,
99
]
. The sets of functionally complete connectives were characterized by E. Post
[
97
]
.

A.3
The algebra of propositions
\mathcal{I}

We will now turn our attention to constructing a set of axioms
\mathcal{I}
for our logical system. From the properties of the connectives (
1
) and (
2
), one can write the laws of logic as a set of
tautologies
,

(Law of identity)

\displaystyle(a\Leftrightarrow a)

(A.10)

(Law of non-contradiction)

\displaystyle\neg(a\wedge\neg a)

(A.11)

(Law of excluded middle)

\displaystyle(a\vee\neg a)

(A.12)

In our formal system, the negation operator satisfies the useful
double negative tautology
50
50

50

The double negation equivalence is not accepted in intuitionist logic.
,

\neg(\neg a)\Leftrightarrow a.

(A.13)

Algebraically, the operators
\wedge
,
\vee
and
\neg
have several interesting properties. These properties will be exploited in the construction of probability theory and so it will be useful to highlight them here.

One can form a
unital
algebra from the space of propositions, which is identified as a
Boolean
algebra
[
89
]
. The
or
relation
\vee
acts as the sum on
\mathcal{A}
, and the
and
relation,
\wedge
, acts as the product. Absolute false,
\mathbf{F}
, is the additive identity, while absolute truth,
\mathbf{T}
, is the multiplicative identity. The following list highlights the algebraic properties of
\wedge
and
\vee
which are considered as axioms in
\mathcal{I}
,

Algebraic properties of
\wedge
and
\vee
—

The conjunction and disjunction have the following algebraic properties,

1.

Commutativity of
\vee
- The
or
relation is commutative,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee b\Leftrightarrow b\vee a.

(A.14)

2.

Associativity of
\vee
- The
or
relation is associative,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee(b\vee c)\Leftrightarrow(a\vee b)\vee c\Leftrightarrow a\vee b\vee c.

(A.15)

3.

Additive identity
- The
or
relation has an additive identity, which is the absolute false
\mathbf{F}
,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee\mathbf{F}\Leftrightarrow a.

(A.16)

4.

Multiplicative identity
- The
and
relation has a multiplicative identity,which is the absolute true
\mathbf{T}
,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge\mathbf{T}\Leftrightarrow a.

(A.17)

5.

Commutativity of
\wedge
- The
and
relation is commutative,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge b\Leftrightarrow b\wedge a.

(A.18)

6.

Associativity of
\wedge
- The
and
relation is associative,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge(b\wedge c)\Leftrightarrow(a\wedge b)\wedge c\Leftrightarrow a\wedge b\wedge c.

(A.19)

7.

Distributivity of
\vee
over
\wedge
- The
or
relation distributes over the
and
relation,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:a\vee(b\wedge c)\Leftrightarrow(a\vee b)\wedge(a\vee c).

(A.20)

8.

Distributivity of
\wedge
over
\vee
- The
and
relation distributes over the
or
relation,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:a\wedge(b\vee c)\Leftrightarrow(a\wedge b)\vee(a\wedge c).

(A.21)

There are several other interesting algebraic relations between
\wedge
,
\vee
and the use of the negation relation
\neg
, which are highlighted in the following table of axioms,

Name

Proposition

Dual

Idempotence

a\wedge a\Leftrightarrow a

a\vee a\Leftrightarrow a

De Morgan’s Laws

\neg(a\wedge b)\Leftrightarrow\neg a\vee\neg b

\neg(a\vee b)\Leftrightarrow\neg a\wedge\neg b

Absorption

(a\wedge b)\vee b\Leftrightarrow b

(a\vee b)\wedge b\Leftrightarrow b

Exclusivity

(a\dot{\vee}b)\Leftrightarrow(a\vee b)\wedge\neg(a\wedge b)

(a\Leftrightarrow b)\Leftrightarrow(a\wedge b)\vee\neg(a\vee b)

Table 3:
Various consequences of the algebra of propositions. The left and right columns are dual in the sense of replacing all
\wedge
’s with
\vee
’s. Cox also provides a nice summary of relations in
[
27
]
.

Together, the space of propositions
\mathcal{A}
with the connectives
\{\wedge,\vee,\neg\}
form the algebra of propositions
(\mathcal{A},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
whose properties were listed in (
A.3
). Since the space of propositions
\mathcal{A}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}
(
A.4
), the algebra of propositions can be brought into correspondence with the algebra of sets over
\mathcal{W}
,

(\mathcal{A},\wedge,\vee,\neg)\cong_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{W},\cap,\cup,\backslash\mathcal{W}),

(A.22)

where
\cap
is the intersection,
\cup
is the union and
\backslash\mathcal{W}
is the complement. This identifies
\mathbf{F}
with the empty set
\emptyset
and
\mathbf{T}
with the entire set
\mathcal{W}
51
51

51

Given the set of worlds
W_{a}
and
W_{b}
in which
a
and
b
are true respectively, it is easy to show that,

\displaystyle W_{a\wedge b}

\displaystyle=W_{a}\cap W_{b}

(A.23)

\displaystyle W_{a\vee b}

\displaystyle=W_{a}\cup W_{b}.

(A.24)

This result is only possible when one adopts the formalism of possible worlds described in
Section 2
. If one allows for a definition of worlds that only contain subsets of
\mathcal{A}
, then the correspondence cannot be made since
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{A}
will not be one-to-one as sets.
. The homomorphism established here is an example of
Stone’s theorem

[
98
]
, which says that any Boolean algebra is isomorphic to a field of sets. The field of sets in particular is a
compact
and
totally disconnected Hausdorff
space, which is called a
Stone space
.

Theorem A.1

(Stone’s representation theorem)
.

Every Boolean algebra is isomorphic to the dual algebra of its associated Stone space.

A.4
The material implication connective
\Rightarrow

The material implication connective
52
52

52

Often the material implication connective is referred to as a conditional, however we will avoid this language here as to not cause confusion by what we mean by conditionals in
Section 9
.

\Rightarrow
is often overlooked in Boolean algebra in favor of studying the
and
and
or
relations. It is much more powerful however, since it leads to several important consequences in logical inference
[
100
,
101
]
. We will highlight some of those ideas here. First, the material implication connective can be written in terms of
or
(or
and
) and negation,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\Rightarrow b\Leftrightarrow\neg a\vee b\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\wedge\neg b).

(A.25)

The semantics for
a\Rightarrow b
is usually written as “if
a
, then
b
.” In general it obeys the following axioms,

Algebraic properties of
\Rightarrow

1.

Distributivity of
\Rightarrow
- The material implication connective distributes over itself,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:\left\{\left[a\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)\right]\Rightarrow\left[(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow(a\Rightarrow c)\right]\right\}.

(A.26)

2.

Transitivity of
\Rightarrow
- The material implication connective is transitive,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:\left\{\left[(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)\right]\Rightarrow(a\Rightarrow c)\right\}.

(A.27)

3.

Reflexivity of
\Rightarrow
- The material implication connective is reflexive,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:(a\Rightarrow a).

(A.28)

4.

Totality of
\Rightarrow
- The material implication connective obeys the totality condition,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:\left\{(a\Rightarrow b)\vee(b\Rightarrow a)\right\}.

(A.29)

Interestingly enough, the propositions
a\wedge(a\Rightarrow b)
, or
b\wedge(b\Rightarrow a)
, contain the equivalent information contained in the proposition
a\wedge b
,

\displaystyle a\wedge(a\Rightarrow b)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a\wedge(\neg a\vee b)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(a\wedge\neg a)\vee(a\wedge b)\Leftrightarrow a\wedge b.

(A.30)

Below is the corresponding truth table for
a\wedge(a\Rightarrow b)
and
b\wedge(b\Rightarrow a)
,

a

b

a\Rightarrow b

b\Rightarrow a

a\wedge(a\Rightarrow b)

b\wedge(b\Rightarrow a)

a\wedge b

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{T}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

\mathbf{F}

Table 4:
Conditional relations between propositions
a
and
b
.

A theorem which will be important later is the following,

Theorem A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the space of propositions. For any propositions,
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
, the distributive property (
A.26
) of the material implication connective (
A.25
) can be written as,

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[a\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(a\wedge b)\Rightarrow c.

(A.31)

Proof.

This is easily proved using (
A.25
) and (
A.8
),

\displaystyle[a\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a\Rightarrow(\neg b\vee c)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg a\vee(\neg b\vee c)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\neg a\vee\neg b)\vee c

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg(a\wedge b)\vee c\Leftrightarrow[(a\wedge b)\Rightarrow c].

(A.32)

∎

Given the commutative nature of the
and
relation, the implications can also be rearranged,

\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[a\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)]\Leftrightarrow[b\Rightarrow(a\Rightarrow c)].

(A.33)

From
theorem 1
we can write a more general result,

Theorem A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the space of propositions. Let
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{N}
be a sequence of propositions. The nested implication over the sequence
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is equivalent to,

[a_{1}\Rightarrow(a_{2}\Rightarrow\dots\Rightarrow(a_{N-1}\Rightarrow a_{N})\cdots)]\Leftrightarrow[(a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}].

(A.34)

Proof.

From
theorem 1
we know that the three inner most terms in the sequence satisfy,

a_{N-2}\Rightarrow(a_{N-1}\Rightarrow a_{N})\Leftrightarrow(a_{N-2}\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}.

(A.35)

Consider the next implication,
a_{N-3}
, outside of these three. Due to the above (
A.35
), it is true that,

a_{N_{3}}\Rightarrow(a_{N-2}\Rightarrow(a_{N-1}\Rightarrow a_{N}))\Leftrightarrow a_{N-3}\Rightarrow\left[(a_{N-2}\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}\right].

(A.36)

The right hand side however has the same form,
a\Rightarrow(b\Rightarrow c)
, from (
A.35
) since
a_{N-2}\wedge a_{N-1}
is also a proposition. Thus it must also be true that,

\displaystyle a_{N_{3}}\Rightarrow(a_{N-2}\Rightarrow(a_{N-1}\Rightarrow a_{N}))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a_{N-3}\Rightarrow\left[(a_{N-2}\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\left[a_{N-3}\wedge(a_{N-2}\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}\right].

(A.37)

Due to the associative (
A.19
) and commutative (
A.18
) properties of
\wedge
, by induction we have,

[a_{1}\Rightarrow(a_{2}\Rightarrow\dots\Rightarrow(a_{N-1}\Rightarrow a_{N})\cdots)]\Leftrightarrow[(a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{N-1})\Rightarrow a_{N}].

(A.38)

∎

McGee
[
102
]
calls this property the
import-export principle
. The material implication defines a partial order on
\mathcal{A}
, which we will discuss in
Section 10.1
.

Principle of explosion —

The material implication connective,
\Rightarrow
, leads to an important principle called
the principle of explosion

[
19
]
, or
ex falso quodlibet
, which means “from falsehood, anything can be proven.” To see this, consider that the contradiction
(a\wedge\neg a)
is true. Then, using
simplification
53
53

53

Simplifcation
is the ability to pull individual propositions out of
a\wedge b
and identify them as true whenever the proposition
a\wedge b
is true itself.
,
a
and
\neg a
are also true on their own. Then, given any statement
b
, the relation
(a\vee b)
must be true, due to
disjuntive introduction
54
54

54

Disjuntive introduction
is the consequence that, given any true statement
a
, one can always add any other statement
b
through disjunction,
a\vee b
, and the resulting proposition will also be true, regardless if
b
is true or false
. But, because
\neg a
we have the
disjuntive syllogism
55
55

55

Disjunctive syllogism
, or
modus tollendo ponens
, is a consequence of the axiom of excluded middle, which says that if one has a disjunction,
a\vee b
, and
\neg a
is known to be true, then
b
must be true, otherwise
a\vee b
is false.
,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:(a\vee b)\wedge\neg a\Leftrightarrow b.

(A.39)

Therefore, from the contradiction
(a\wedge\neg a)
, we’ve established that any
b
is true, i.e.
(a\wedge\neg a)\Rightarrow b
. The term
explosion
signifies the fact that, once one contradiction is allowed in
\mathcal{A}
, anything can be proven to be true.

A.5
Deductive inference rules
\mathcal{Z}

In our simple system there are at most
24
valid inference rules in
\mathcal{Z}
which are distinct
[
7
]
. These are often called
syllogisms
which constitute the basic form of a logical argument that were first organized by Aristotle. There are
256
distinct syllogisms in total, meaning that
232
of them are
formal fallacies
, which constitute invalid forms of reasoning. Below is a table of some commonly used rules of inference,

Connective

Inference Rule

Semantics (
\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
)

\Rightarrow

Modus ponens

a,a\Rightarrow b\vdash b

Modus tollens

\neg b,a\Rightarrow b\vdash\neg a

\wedge

Conjunction introduction

a,b\vdash a\wedge b

Conjunction elimination

a\vdash a\vee b

Simplification

a\wedge b,\vdash a

\vee

Disjunction introduction

a\vdash a\vee b

Disjunction elimination

a\Rightarrow c,b\Rightarrow c,a\vee b\vdash c

Modus tollendo ponens

a\vee b,\neg a\vdash b

\Rightarrow,\vee

Constructive dilemma

a\Rightarrow b,c\Rightarrow d,a\vee c\vdash b\vee d

Table 5:
Common inference rules for propositional calculus

These and other inference rules allow one to construct proofs for stated theorems. Proofs are examples of logical arguments, which are a sequence of propositions
\Gamma=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\}
, such that each proposition
a_{i}\in\Gamma
is either an axiom from
\mathcal{I}
, or it is a tautology, such as the laws (
A.12
), or it is an instance of
modus ponens
.

Implication elimination —

The material implication connective gives rise to a standard rule of inference called
modus ponens
(more completely,
modus ponendo ponens
), or “mode that by affirming affirms”
[
6
]
. This is essentially the statement that if a proposition
a
implies
b
, i.e.
a\Rightarrow b
, and
a
is true, then
b
must also be true. A standard form of the argument is as follows,

If Werner is a physicist, then he understands quantum mechanics.

Werner is a physicist.

Therefore, Werner understands quantum mechanics.

Identifying “Werner is a physicst” as “
a
” and “Werner understands quantum mechanics” as “
b
”, the semantic version of the above is written,

\displaystyle\mathbf{MP}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:a\Rightarrow b,a\vdash b

.

(A.40)

Reductio ad absurdum —

From the properties of the implication arrow we can write the following theorem, which is called formally a
contraposition

[
6
]
,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:(a\Rightarrow b)\Leftrightarrow(\neg b\Rightarrow\neg a).

(A.41)

This situation allows us to prove propositions by contradiction, which is a special case of what is known as
reductio ad absurdum
, or “argument from absurdity.” If we first assume that
a
is true, then the statement
a\Rightarrow b
is only true whenever
b
is true as well. Instead, if we assume that
b
is false, and this leads to
\neg b\Rightarrow\neg a
being false, then it must be the case that
b
is actually true.

Sequents as tautologies —

Proofs, such as
\Gamma\vdash b
, can be written as tautologies by first writing the antecedent as a conjunction,

\Gamma\Leftrightarrow a_{1}\wedge a_{2}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{n},

(A.42)

which together with a theorem
b
forms the statement
\Gamma\Rightarrow b
. These statements can be collected into the set of theorems for a given proposition space
\mathcal{O}
.

Summary of propositional calculus —

In this section we have briefly explored the structure
\mathcal{D}=\left\{\mathcal{A},\Omega,\mathcal{I},\mathcal{Z}\right\}
that we sought to define in the introduction. Additional structure such as the set of all possible worlds
W
will be appended to a set
\mathcal{T}
which can then be added to
\mathcal{D}=\left\{\mathcal{A},\Omega,\mathcal{I},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}\right\}
. Proofs are special propositions which exist as a collection of subsets of
\mathcal{A}
.

A.6
First-order predicate logic

While the algebra of propositions serves as a useful foundation, in physics we are often interested in the use of
predicates
. First-order predicate logic introduces the notion of a predicate, together with two
quantors
,
\forall
and
\exists
. It was first formally developed by Frege in his
Begriffsschrift

[
101
]
. It was also independently developed by Pierce
[
103
]
. The concepts of quantification are closely related with work that goes back to Aristotle in his
De interpretatione

[
7
]
.

A predicate is defined as,

Definition A.7
.

A predicate,
A(x)
, is a proposition valued function for some variable
x
.

In the broadest sense, the variable
x
can be assigned to any element of the space of words
\Sigma^{*}
. Likewise, a predicate is also an element of the space of words
A(x)\in\Sigma^{*}
, in which the variable
x
appears as part of the word. We can then identify the
space of predicates
as some suitable subspace of the space of words,
\mathcal{P}\subset\Sigma^{*}
.

It is important to note that a predicate such as
A(x)
is
not
a proposition. It only becomes a proposition once the argument
x
has been specified, otherwise
x
remains
free
in
A(x)

[
5
]
. For example, the predicate
A(x)
could be, “
x
is mortal.” Depending on what we substitute for
x
the predicate
A(x)
will inherit a meaning. If for
x
we put “Socrates,” then
A(x)
becomes the proposition “Socrates is mortal.” If however we put for
x
, “the Heisenberg uncertainty principle,” then
A(x)
is nonsense. Thus, there is a range of applicability of
A(x)
with respect to
x

[
19
]
.

Besides inserting a singular value for
x
in
A(x)
, there are several other ways in which one can construct a proposition from a predicate. Two of them involve the use of the
universal
and
existential
quantors,
\forall
and
\exists
, that were introduced in the algebraic axioms for Boolean algebra. The universal quantor can be defined as,

Definition A.8
.

Let
A(x)
be a predicate for the variable
x
. The universal quantor
\forall
is defined as,

\forall:\Sigma^{*}\times\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}.

(A.43)

It does this by quantifying the variable
x
,

\forall x:A(x)\Leftrightarrow[A(a)\wedge A(b)\wedge A(c)\wedge\dots],

(A.44)

which reads, “for all
x
,
A(x)
is true,” i.e.
A
is true independent of
x
.

Likewise, the existential quantor
\exists
is defined as,

Definition A.9
.

Let
A(x)
be a predicate for the variable
x
. The existential quantor
\exists
is defined as,

\exists:\Sigma^{*}\times\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}.

(A.45)

We can quantify
x
through the statement,

\exists x:A(x)\Leftrightarrow[A(a)\vee A(b)\vee A(c)\vee\dots],

(A.46)

which reads, “there exists at least one
x
, such that
A(x)
is true.”

Like propositions, one can generate new predicates from old ones using the binary relations in
table 2
. From these relations, the existential quantor can be defined in terms of the universal quantor as,

\exists x:A(x)\Leftrightarrow\neg\left(\forall x:\neg A(x)\right).

(A.47)

The universal quantor is then,

\forall x:\neg A(x)\Leftrightarrow\neg\left(\exists x:A(x)\right).

(A.48)

One can also define the uniqueness quantor
\exists!
, which when applied to a predicate like,
\exists!x:A(x)
reads “there exists one and only one
x
such that
A(x)
is true.” The uniqueness quantor can be expressed as,

\exists!x:A(x)\Leftrightarrow\exists x[A(x)\wedge\neg\exists y:[A(y)\wedge y\neq x]],

(A.49)

i.e., “there exists an
x
such that
A(x)
is true and there exists no
y
such that
A(y)
is true when
y
is not equal to
x
.”

The formalism can easily be extended to predicates of several variables,
A(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
. For predicates of several variables, such as
A(x,y)
, one can generate a new predicate through the use of the quantors. For example, the predicate
A(x)
can be generated from
A(x,y)
by specifying
y
through either
\forall
or
\exists
,

\displaystyle A(x)\Leftrightarrow[\exists y:A(x,y)]\Leftrightarrow[A(x,a)\vee A(x,b)\vee A(x,c)\vee\dots].

(A.50)

Here the variable
y
is called
bound
, while the
x
remains
free
. In practice one should be careful with the order of the use of quantors, since in general,

\forall x:\exists y:A(x,y)\not\Leftrightarrow\exists y:\forall x:A(x,y).

(A.51)

The above propositions are necessarily different since,

\displaystyle\forall x:\exists y:A(x,y)\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle[A(a,a)\vee A(a,b)\vee A(a,c)\vee\dots]

\displaystyle\wedge

\displaystyle\,[A(b,a)\vee A(b,b)\vee A(b,c)\vee\dots]

\displaystyle\wedge

\displaystyle\,[A(c,a)\vee A(c,b)\vee A(c,c)\vee\dots]

\displaystyle\wedge

\displaystyle\,\dots,

(A.52)

where as,

\displaystyle\exists y:\forall x:A(x,y)\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle[A(a,a)\wedge A(b,a)\wedge A(c,a)\wedge\dots]

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle\,[A(a,b)\wedge A(b,b)\wedge A(c,b)\wedge\dots]

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle\,[A(a,c)\wedge A(b,c)\wedge A(c,c)\wedge\dots]

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle\,\dots,

(A.53)

There is however an implication given by switching the quantor,

\exists y:\forall x:A(x,y)\Rightarrow\forall y:\exists x:A(x,y).

(A.54)

This can easily be proved by using the form of (
A.44
) and (
A.46
). There are also the useful axioms involving conjunction and disjunction,

\displaystyle\mathbf{PC}

\displaystyle\forall x:A(x)\wedge B(x)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow[\forall x:A(x)]\wedge[\forall x:B(x)]

(A.55)

\displaystyle\mathbf{PD}

\displaystyle[\forall x:A(x)]\vee[\forall x:B(x)]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\forall x:A(x)\vee B(x).

(A.56)

Given the definition of a predicate (
A.7
), the quantors (
A.8
), (
A.9
), and any axioms such a
\mathbf{PC}
and
\mathbf{PD}
, we establish what is called a
first-order
predicate logic. It is of first-order since it does not concern predicate valued predicates, which would be a predicate logic of
second-order
. One can construct higher-order predicate logics from first-order ones
[
9
]
, but we will not discuss this further.

One can also adopt a set of new inference rules concerning the quantors. Some examples of these are the following,

Quantor

Inference Rule

Semantics (
\forall A(x),B(x),C(x)\in\mathcal{P}
)

\forall

Universal generalization

A(a/x)\Rightarrow\forall x:A(x)

Universal instantiation

\forall x:A(x)\Rightarrow A(a/x)

\exists

Existential generalization

A(a/x)\Rightarrow\exists x:A(x)

Existential instantiation

\exists x:A(x)\Rightarrow A(a/x)

Table 6:
Common inference rules for first-order logic.

The notation
A(a/x)
means to replace the free variable
x
everywhere in
A(x)
with
a
. There are restrictions on how one must analyze these formulas due to the behavior of free and bound variables
56
56

56

For example, if one has the predicate
A(x,y)
over the two variables
x
and
y
, one cannot use an inference rule such as
\exists x:A(x,y)\Rightarrow A(a/x,y)
, and replace
a
with some function of
y
. This will leave open the possibility for contradiction. In general, one cannot substitute free variables with bound ones when using these rules.

The summary thus far —

In a sense, the space of predicates,
\mathcal{P}\subset\Sigma^{*}
, together with the quantifiers
\forall
and
\exists
is more general than the space of propositions
\mathcal{A}
. Thus, we can organize our predicate logic as an extension of the system
\mathcal{D}
by incorporating the space of predicates,
\mathcal{P}\subset\Sigma^{*}
, together with the quantors
\mathcal{Q}=\{\forall,\exists\}
. The axioms such as
\mathbf{PC}
and
\mathbf{PD}
can be collected as a set
\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}
, which can be appended to our list of propositional axioms
\mathcal{I}
from
Section 4
and
2.5
so that
\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{p}=\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}
. Likewise the set of inference rules from
Section 6
can be appended with the set
\mathcal{Z}_{\forall,\exists}
, which could contain rules from (
6
), so that we define
\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{p}=\mathcal{Z}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{\forall,\exists}
. Finally, any additional structure introduced with respect to the predicate calculus we label
\mathcal{T}_{\forall,\exists}
, which can then be appended to the structures in
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{p}=\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\forall,\exists}
. We call this system
\mathcal{D}_{p}=\left\{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{A},\Omega,\mathcal{I}_{p},\mathcal{Z}_{p},\mathcal{T}_{p}\right\}
.

The semantics that one could employ with this framework is rather limited. In the next few sections we will attempt to extend the system
\mathcal{D}
in order to incorporate a more complex semantics. This involves the incorporation of
modalities
as well as
conditional logic
.

A.7
Modal logic

In this section we will briefly survey some of the basic properties of modal logic. Modal logic
[
19
,
104
,
105
]
dates back to Aristotle, who studied the semantics associated to words like “necessary” and “possible.” This can be formalized into an extension of classical logic by identifying a
modality
, which is a term used in linguistics that typically refers to the
intention
of the speaker.

A modality is a phrase that, when applied to a statement, generates a new statement that addresses the
mode of truth
of the original statement. Depending on the modality one is using, the subject can range from temporal logic
[
106
]
to ethics and morality (Deontic logic
[
107
]
) or to the logic concerning beliefs (Doxastic logic
[
108
]
). Each of these programs has in common a new set of logical operators which have the character of the
universal
and
existential
quantors, (
A.8
) and (
A.9
), and are given by the symbols
\square
and
\diamondsuit
.

While the inference framework should ultimately be agnostic with respect to what modalities one is using, we will develop the theory of conditionals using the
alethic
approach in which
\square
stands for “necessary” and
\diamondsuit
stands for “possibly.” In particular, the
necessary
and
possibly
modalities generate statements such as,
\square p
, which reads “necessarily,
p
,” and,
\diamondsuit p
, which reads “possibly,
p
.”

There are several axiomatic systems that one can impose concerning the modal operators. Most of these systems were developed by Lewis and Langford in their book “Symbolic Logic”
[
19
]
. The most basic of these axioms identifies a dual relationship between
\square
and
\diamondsuit
,

Definition A.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a space of propositions. The modal operators
\square
and
\diamondsuit
represent the modalities “necessary” and “possibly,” and are dual to each other, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a\Leftrightarrow\neg\diamondsuit\neg a

(A.57)

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit a\Leftrightarrow\neg\square\neg a.

(A.58)

According to the definition (
A.10
), the statement, “the particle will
necessarily
go through slit A or slit B,” is identical to the statement, “it is not
possible
that the particle will not go through slit A or slit B.” Likewise, the statement, “it is possible that the particle will go through slit A,” is identical to the statement, “it is not necessarily true that the particle will not go through slit A.” Both of these equivalences seem intuitive, and are hardly controversial. As we construct more complicated modal logics however, the statements will become less intuitive, and perhaps controversial.

Modal theory incorporates the
possible worlds semantics
through what are called
accessibility relations
. An accessibility relation is a relation between two worlds,
w
and
w^{\prime}
that obeys the following axioms,

Definition A.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a countable set of propositions and let
W
be the set of possible worlds over
\mathcal{A}
. An accessibility relation
R\subseteq W\times W\rightarrow W
, is a binary relation between two worlds such that, if
\square a
is true in world
w_{1}
and
a
is true in
w_{2}
, then we say that
w_{2}
is accessible to
w_{1}
, i.e.,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\forall w_{1}:[\nu(\square a,w_{1})\Leftrightarrow\forall w_{2}:R(w_{1},w_{2})\Rightarrow\nu(a,w_{2})].

(A.59)

The proposition
R(w_{1},w_{2})
defines the statement “
w_{2}
is accessible to
w_{1}
,” and is sometimes written as
R(w_{1},w_{2})\Leftrightarrow w_{1}Rw_{2}
. Given the
actual world
, denoted by
\tilde{w}
, the statement that a proposition
a\in\mathcal{A}
is necessarily true,
\square a
, means that it is true in all possible worlds. This can be written as,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a\Leftrightarrow[\forall w\in W:\tilde{w}Rw\Rightarrow\nu(a,w)].

(A.60)

Likewise, we say that
a
is possible,
\diamondsuit a
, if there exists a world
w\in W
such that,

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit a\Leftrightarrow[\exists w\in W:\tilde{w}Rw\Rightarrow\nu(a,w)].

(A.61)

From here, different modal logics can be formed by constructing additional axioms on the behavior of the universal and existential operators
\square
and
\diamondsuit
. Some are more controversial than others among logicians and we will introduce them in an order of their suggested controversy. Among the different families of modal logics are the ones first developed by Lewis
[
19
]
.

The simplest modal logic is typically labeled by
\mathbf{K}
, which is named after Saul Kripke
[
91
]
. Together with the duality axiom (
A.10
), it adopts the following additional axioms to standard propositional logic,

Axiom A.1

(The logic
K
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the space of propositions and let
\square
and
\diamondsuit
be the modal operators defined in (
A.10
). We call a modal logic of type
K
if it obeys the following distribution axiom,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle\forall a,b,\in\mathcal{A}:\square(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow(\square a\Rightarrow\square b),

(A.62)

together with the necessitation rule,

\vdash A\vDash\square A.

(A.63)

The necessitation rule reads, “if
A
is a theorem, then
\square A
is also a theorem.” The proposition
\square(a\Rightarrow b)
is called the
strict implication
. An additional set of axioms that are typically adopted are given in the following list,

Axioms A.1

(The axioms
\mathbf{D}
,
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{S}_{4}
and
\mathbf{S}_{5}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the space of propositions and let
\square
and
\diamondsuit
be the modal operators defined in (
A.10
). The following axioms are represented by the letters in the left-hand column,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a\Rightarrow\diamondsuit a.

(A.64)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a\Rightarrow a.

(A.65)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{4}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a\Rightarrow\square\square a.

(A.66)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{5}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit a\Rightarrow\square\diamondsuit a.

(A.67)

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:a\Rightarrow\square\diamondsuit a.

(A.68)

Axiom
\mathbf{D}
is hardly controversial, since for any proposition
a
it implies that “if
a
is necessarily true, then
a
is possible.” In ethical circles the universal and existential operators take the modalities “obligatory” and “permissible,” hence axiom (
A.64
) entails statements such as, “if
a
is obligatory, then
a
is permissible.” Axiom
\mathbf{M}
implies
57
57

57

In Deontic logic axiom
\mathbf{M}
is not accepted since it leads to an “ought,is” fallacy, i.e. “if
a
ought to be true, then it is true.” Instead, axiom
\mathbf{D}
, which is much weaker, is chosen.
, “if
a
is necessarily true, then
a
is true.” The system
\mathbf{M}
contains
\mathbf{D}
as a subsystem due to the following corollary,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:a\Rightarrow\diamondsuit a.

(A.69)

Due to (
A.10
) there is also an associated corollary to axiom
\mathbf{S}_{4}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit\diamondsuit a\Rightarrow\diamondsuit a.

(A.70)

The definitions (
A.66
) and (
A.70
) allow us to reduce any statement which contains a string of either
\square
’s or
\diamondsuit
’s to a single one. Likewise, one can extend a single
\square
or
\diamondsuit
to a string of them.

Axiom
\mathbf{S}_{5}
(
A.67
) is the subject of some controversy, since it allows one to form variants of the ontological argument of Plantinga
58
58

58

The ontological argument is one example of
a priori
reasoning which, as Hume argued
[
109
]
, is invalid and unscientific. In science we cannot
prove
that things exist, we can only demonstrate that theories are inconsistent.
. There are plenty of reasons however to reject axiom
\mathbf{S}_{5}
. One argument involves the use of axiom
\mathbf{B}
(
A.68
). Axiom
\mathbf{B}
defines a sort of “in-between” for the axioms
\mathbf{S}_{4}
and
\mathbf{S}_{5}
, since the system
{S}_{5}
can be constructed by adding
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{S}_{4}
. Axiom
\mathbf{B}
reads, “if
a
is true, then
a
is necessarily possible,” which seems reasonable enough. The corollary to axiom
\mathbf{B}
however can be written,

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit\square a\Rightarrow a.

(A.71)

The corollary (
A.71
) states that, “if
a
is possibly necessary, then
a
is true.” This seems to invoke a fallacy, however it is simply the result of accepting axiom
\mathbf{B}
(
A.68
) which seems rather reasonable. The problem is that axiom
\mathbf{B}
is really a bad way of writing the following,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square(a\Rightarrow\diamondsuit a).

(A.72)

Together, axioms
\mathbf{K}
(
A.62
) and
\mathbf{M}
(
A.65
) imply the statement, “it is necessarily true that if
a
is true, then
a
is possible.” As pointed out in the Stanford encyclopedia of philosophy
[
110
]
, the confusion between axiom
\mathbf{B}
and the above statement (
A.72
) is due to the syntactical ambiguities used in the English language. In order to avoid accepting
\mathbf{B}
, and ultimately
\mathbf{S}_{5}
, we should instead evaluate the merits of
\mathbf{B}
using the corollary
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, which is easily rejected.

It is also often useful to adopt analogous axioms to those of the predicate quantors over conjunction (
A.55
) and disjunction (
A.56
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{NC}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:\square(a\wedge b)\Rightarrow(\square a\wedge\square b),

(A.73)

\displaystyle\mathbf{CN}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:(\square a\wedge\square b)\Rightarrow\square(a\wedge b),

(A.74)

\displaystyle\mathbf{ND}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:(\square a\vee\square b)\Rightarrow\square(a\vee b).

(A.75)

Adoption of
\mathbf{NC}
and
\mathbf{CN}
together imply the equivalence in (
A.55
). Some programs of modal logic however will adopt
\mathbf{NC}
but not the converse
\mathbf{CN}
59
59

59

For a history of first-order modal logics see
[
111
]
.
.

Together the axioms (
A.62
), (
A.64
), (
A.65
), (
A.66
) and (
A.67
) determine the set of
normal modal logics
. Depending on the system adopted, the accessibility relation (
A.11
) accumulates certain properties. These are the following,

(Reflexive)

\displaystyle\forall w\in W:wRw.

(A.76)

(Symmetric)

\displaystyle\forall w,v\in W:wRv\Rightarrow vRw.

(A.77)

(Transitive)

\displaystyle\forall w,v,u:(wRv\wedge vRu)\Rightarrow wRu.

(A.78)

(Serial)

\displaystyle\forall w\in W:\exists v\in W:wRu.

(A.79)

(Euclidean)

\displaystyle\forall w,v,u\in W:(wRv\wedge wRu)\Rightarrow vRu.

(A.80)

For one, if
\mathbf{M}
is adopted, then
R
becomes reflexive. If
\mathbf{D}
is adopted then
R
becomes
serial
. In
\mathbf{S}_{4}
,
R
is reflexive and transitive and in
\mathbf{S}_{5}
,
R
is reflexive and Euclidean.

Typically there is only one additional rule of inference that is added to modal systems. This is an extension of
modus ponens
(
A.5
) which can be called a
modal modus ponens
and has the following semantics,

\square(a\Rightarrow b),\square a\vdash\square b.

(A.81)

Like we did with first-order predicate logic, we can compile the set of basic properties of modal logic into our
deductive system
in the following way. First, we can collect the modal operators into a set
\Omega_{\square,\diamondsuit}=\{\square,\diamondsuit\}
which can be added to the connectives
\Omega
such that,
\Omega\rightarrow\Omega_{m}=\Omega\cup\{\square,\diamondsuit\}
. Any set of modal axioms, such as the ones in (
A.1
), can be collected into a set
\mathcal{I_{\square,\diamondsuit}}
. These can then be added to the axioms
\mathcal{I}_{p}
so that,
\mathcal{I}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{m}=\mathcal{I}_{p}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
. We can also collect any rules of inference, including the modal modus ponens (
A.81
), into a set
\mathcal{Z}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
which can be added to
\mathcal{Z}_{p}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{m}=\mathcal{Z}_{p}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
.

Some modal systems introduce different structures in the form of
frames
,
models
and various maps such as the accessibility relation. We will not attempt to classify all of these different approaches and instead refer to these additional structures as belonging to some set
\mathcal{T}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
which gets appended to
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
. Then, our modal-predicate system is given by the tuple,

\mathcal{D}_{m}=\{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{A},\Omega_{m},\mathcal{I}_{m},\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathcal{T}_{m}\}.

(A.82)

There are some technical, and perhaps philosophical, difficulties with applying a predicate calculus to modal statements. This was first raised as a point by Quine
[
88
]
and has since been a continuing problem. These problems can be compounded depending on the axioms that are accepted as part of
\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
. We will not attempt to survey the approaches to these issues here and instead assume that whatever structures are ultimately imposed in
\mathcal{T}
, that they be consistent with those axioms adopted in
\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
.

Which axioms in
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
should we accept as part of our inference framework? —

The subject matter presented in this section is only a small introduction to the vast array of different approaches to incorporating modalities. At this point, we leave the sets
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
open so that the inference framework can accommodate any additional axioms or structures one wishes to use. In order to perhaps narrow the list of axioms, we will study the approaches for how to add general conditional statements to our system.

A.8
Conditional logic

Our goal in this section is to develop a modern theory of conditionals as it pertains to deductive inference. Conditional statements are common in English, and typically come in three forms;
indicative
(or
matter-of-fact
),
subjunctive
and
counter-factual
. An
indicative
conditional is one which can be stated in terms of objective reality as it is. Subjunctive conditionals express desires such as, “if only it would
a
, then it would
b
.” Of course the adverb “would” suggests that
a
and
b
refer to things in the future, which is already a problematic situation to deal with.

Counterfactuals on the other hand are not statements which reflect actual reality
[
112
]
. They typically deal with hypothetical statements about the world as it could possibly be if certain objective facts were actually false. For example,

“If Heisenberg hadn’t hated wave mechanics, no one would believe the Copenhagen interpretation.”

It is not obvious how one would access the
truth
of such a statement.

Attempts to construct a well defined theory of conditionals in deductive inference have mostly led to confusion
[
113
,
23
,
29
,
114
]
. It is still not a settled question in deductive logic. There are several reasons for this, but the main one, and coincidentally the reason why such an idea cannot be fully defined in deductive inference, will be discussed at the end of this section. Despite these difficulties, there are certain situations in which a special class of conditional statements can be well defined in a deductive setting, which we will attempt to classify.

Most discussions of conditionals begin with a reference to F. Ramsey’s “test”
[
50
]
, where he specifies a criterion that should hold for any notion of a conditional,

“If two people are arguing “if
p
will
q
?” and are both in doubt as to
p
, they are adding
p
hypothetically to their stock of knowledge and arguing on that basis about
q
; so that in a sense “if
p
,
q
” and “if
p
,
\neg q
” are contradictories.”(Ramsey, 1929)

This passage points out that the negation with respect to conditional statements, “if
p
,
q
”, should only affect the consequent
q
, and not the antecedent
p
. This is because the statement “if
p
will
q
?” is already assuming that
p
is true. This condition is obvious, which is why Ramsey only mentioned it in passing. Any system which attempts to construct a conditional must in the very least adhere to this requirement.

One might assume that the material implication connective
\Rightarrow
is the type of conditional which properly encapsulates matter-of-fact conditionals and counterfactuals and which eventually leads to conditional probabilities. This however is incorrect, since it automatically violates the Ramsey test
60
60

60

For some examples see Bradley
[
115
]
.
,

\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:\neg(a\Rightarrow b)\not\Leftrightarrow a\Rightarrow\neg b.

(A.83)

It also causes inconsistencies with the sum and product rules developed for probabilities in the next chapter. One can see this by examining the consequences of the equivalences in (
A.25
). Despite these problems, several authors have attempted to justify this idea, including
[
116
,
114
,
23
,
117
]
, however the definitions only coincide in special cases.

In
[
118
,
119
,
120
]
, R. Stalnaker et. al. develops an extension of modal logic to include a new connective (or relation) which he calls the
corner
, and is written with a greater than symbol, i.e.
a>b
. This new connective is meant to represent what we would intuitively understand as a conditional statement, “assuming
a
is true, what do we believe about the truth of
b
?” Stalnaker gives a criterion for how one would access the truth of a proposition when it is conditioned on another,

“First, add the antecedent (hypothetically) to your stock of beliefs; second, make whatever adjustments are required to maintain consistency (without modifying the hypothetical belief in the antecedent); finally, consider whether of not the consequent is true.” (Stalnaker, 1970)

One may refer to the above construction as a
principle of minimal distortion
of a world
w\in W
. One procedure for evaluating
b>a
is as follows. First we define a model
[
22
,
121
]
,

Definition A.12
.

A model,
M=\left\langle W,f,P\right\rangle
, is a tuple containing the set of all possible worlds
W
, together with a function
f:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}(W)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(W)
which is called a selection function, and a mapping
P:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(W)
.

The selection function defined above takes in a proposition
a\in\mathcal{A}
and a possible world
w\in W
and returns either the empty set
\emptyset
, if no worlds in
W
are such that
a
is true, or it returns the set
\{w\}
if
a
is true in
w
, or it returns a set of worlds which are
altered versions
of
w
in the most
minimal
way possible.
61
61

61

As an example of a definition for the function
f
when
a
is not true in
w
, but is true in some worlds of
W
, consider the following function
\bar{\nu}_{a}:\mathcal{A}\times W\times W\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, defined in terms of (
A.2
),

\bar{\nu}_{a}(w,w^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{matrix}1&\Leftrightarrow&\nu(a,w)\Leftrightarrow\nu(a,w^{\prime})\\
0&\Leftrightarrow&\nu(a,w)\not\Leftrightarrow\nu(a,w^{\prime})\end{matrix}\right..

(A.84)

The above function compares the truth value of the proposition
a\in\mathcal{A}
and returns
1
if the truth value is the same in both worlds
w
and
w^{\prime}
, or returns zero if it is not. We can compare the differences in all truth values between two worlds
w,w^{\prime}\in W
by summing over all propositions,
\bar{\nu}(w,w^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\bar{\nu}_{a}(w,w^{\prime}).
Such a function maps to the natural numbers,
\bar{\nu}:W\times W\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. Now we can define the world
w_{a}
as the one which minimizes
\bar{\nu}
over the subset
W_{a}
,

w_{a}(w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\min_{w^{\prime}\in W_{a}}\bar{\nu}(w,w^{\prime}).

(A.85)

There may be more than one world
w\in W
which satisfies this criteria. We call the collection of worlds which satisfy (
A.85
)
W_{a}^{w}\subseteq W
, and are the result of the function
f(a,w)
when
\nu(a,w)
is false but there exist some worlds
w^{\prime}\in W
in which
\nu(a,w^{\prime})
is true.

Any sentence
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{A}
which is true in the world
w\in W
can be written
\vDash^{M}_{w}\Gamma
. If
\Gamma
is true in all possible worlds we write
\vDash^{M}\Gamma
, which is the same as the statement
\square\Gamma
. The truth of material implication with respect to the world
w\in W
can be written,

\vDash^{M}_{w}(a\Rightarrow b)\Leftrightarrow[(\vDash^{M}_{w}a)\Rightarrow(\vDash^{M}_{w}b)].

(A.86)

This is a result we would expect, since the material implication is well-defined within any world. For the conditional however, we find the following,

\vDash^{M}_{w}(a>b)\Leftrightarrow f(a,W_{a})\subseteq W_{b}.

(A.87)

This demands that whatever worlds in which
a
is true must be a subset of the worlds in which
b
is true, otherwise we allow ex falso quodlibet.

A conditional as defined by Stalnaker
[
21
]
is the following,

Definition A.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a set of propositions. For any propositions,
a
and
b
in
\mathcal{A}
, we define the conditional relation
a>b
, which reads “
b
given
a
.” The conditional relation is defined in terms of the universal and existential modal operators as,

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\square a

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg a>a

(A.88)

\displaystyle\forall a\,\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit a

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\neg(a>\neg a).

(A.89)

The first condition may seem strange intuitively, however it is simply a proposition which states that
a
is necessarily true, if it is still true in the face of its contrary. This represents a state of complete certainty with respect to beliefs about the proposition
a
. What’s more important is the compliment relation, which says that
a
is possible, if the conditional statement
\neg a|a
is false. For all situations in which the truth of
a
is not absolutely certain,
a
will always be possible. The definitions in (
A.13
) are easily seen to be consistent with (
A.87
) since for
\square a
,
\neg a
is not true in any world and hence
f(\neg a,W)=\emptyset
. For
\diamondsuit a
,
a
must be true in at least one possible world, so that
f(a,W)\neq\emptyset
.

In order to avoid confusion with the greater than symbol applied to numbers, we will write conditionals using the
solidus
“
|
” so that the statement
a>b
is written
b|a
.
Depending on the type of conditional logic one wishes to develop, one can adopt some subset of the following axioms,

Axioms A.2
.

The following list gives possible axioms for a system of conditional logic. The names in parenthesis correspond to the convention given by Chellas
[
22
]
. The ones which begin with
\mathbf{S}
are defined by Stalnaker
[
118
]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{SN}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:\square(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow(b|a)

(A.90)

\displaystyle\mathbf{SP}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\diamondsuit a\Rightarrow[b|a\Rightarrow\neg(\neg b|a)]

(A.91)

\displaystyle\mathbf{SD}

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:b\vee c|a\Rightarrow(b|a)\vee(c|a)

(A.92)

\displaystyle\mathbf{SC}

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[(b|a)\wedge(a|b)]\Rightarrow[(c|a)\Rightarrow(c|b)]

(A.93)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{RCEA})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[a\Leftrightarrow b]\Rightarrow[(c|a)\Leftrightarrow(c|b)]

(A.94)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{RCEC})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[a\Leftrightarrow b]\Rightarrow[(c|a)\Leftrightarrow(c|b)]

(A.95)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{RCM})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow[(a|c)\Rightarrow(b|c)]

(A.96)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{RCR})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{A}:[(a\wedge b)\Rightarrow c]\Rightarrow[((a|d)\wedge(b|d))\Rightarrow(c|d)]

(A.97)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{RCK})

\displaystyle[(a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{n})\Rightarrow b]\Rightarrow[(a_{1}|c)\wedge\dots\wedge(a_{n}|c)]\Rightarrow(b|c)

(A.98)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{MP})

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:b|a\Rightarrow a\Rightarrow b

(A.99)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{CM})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[a\wedge b|c]\Rightarrow[(a|c)\wedge(b|c)]

(A.100)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{CC})

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}:[(a|c)\wedge(b|c)]\Rightarrow[a\wedge b|c]

(A.101)

Stalnaker defines his conditional logic
[
118
]
through the axioms
\mathbf{MP}
and
\mathbf{SN}+\mathbf{SP}+\mathbf{SD}+\mathbf{SC}
.
Chellas
[
22
]
showed that the class of conditional logics
CK
defined by axioms
\mathbf{RCEA}
and
\mathbf{RCK}
are decideable and are determined by the class of standard frames (
A.12
).

One important take away of these axioms is the sequence of entailment with respect to the strict implication and the normal implication,

\displaystyle\mathbf{SN}+\mathbf{MP}

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}:\square(a\Rightarrow b)\Rightarrow(b|a)\Rightarrow(a\Rightarrow b)

.

(A.102)

This demonstrates that our intuition about the implication arrow representing conditional statements is not totally off base, however they are not the same. The conditional
b|a
implies the implication
a\Rightarrow b
, however the antecedant
a
in
b|a
is always assumed to be true, whereas in the implication
a
could be false in an example of
ex falso quodlibet
.

While there are plenty of axioms to choose from, the inference framework we adopt here will only need a few. The framework should at least include axioms
\mathbf{SD}
,
\mathbf{RCR}
,
\mathbf{CM}
and
\mathbf{CC}
. These properties are all that we will need to construct our inductive inference of the next chapter.

We can collect the solidus into our set of connectives,
\Omega_{m}\rightarrow\Omega_{c}=\Omega_{m}\cup\{|\}
. In total we have,
\Omega_{m}=\Omega\cup\{\square,\diamondsuit\}\cup\{|\}
. We can also collect any axioms concerning “
|
” into a set
\mathcal{I}_{|}
, as well as any additional structure such as the model
M
(
A.12
) into a set
\mathcal{T}_{|}
. Our complete list of axioms is then,
\mathcal{I}_{c}=\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}\cup\mathcal{I}_{|}
.

Summary of our deductive system —

Our deductive system can be written as the tuple,
\mathcal{D}_{c}=\left\{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{A},\Omega_{c},\mathcal{I}_{c},\mathcal{Z}_{c},\mathcal{T}_{c}\right\}
whose elements are the following,

1.

(
Predicates
) - The set
\mathcal{P}\subset\Sigma^{*}
of predicates for a particular universe of discourse.

2.

(
Quantors
) - The set of quantification operators
\{\forall,\exists\}
(
A.8
),(
A.9
).

3.

(
Propositions
) - The set of all
propositions

\mathcal{A}
for a particular universe of discourse.

4.

(
Connectives
) - The set of
connectives

\Omega
containing the set of generators
\{\wedge,\vee\,\neg\}
, the
modal operators

\{\square,\diamondsuit\}
and the
conditional operator

|
.

5.

(
Axioms
) - The set of
propositional axioms

\mathcal{I}
together with the axioms involving the modal operators
\mathcal{I}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
, the axioms concerning the quantors
\mathcal{I}_{\forall,\exists}
and the axioms concerning the conditional operator
\mathcal{I}_{|}
.

6.

(
Inference Rules
) - The set of
inference rules

\mathcal{Z}
for propositional calculus, together with any inference rules adopted for predicate calculus,
\mathcal{Z}_{\forall,\exists}
, and inference rules for the modal operators
\mathcal{Z}_{\square,\diamondsuit}
and the conditional operator
\mathcal{Z}_{|}
.

7.

(
Additional structure
) - The set
\mathcal{T}
of any additional structures such as the set of possible worlds (
A.3
), frames, models (
A.12
), accessibility relations (
A.11
), etc.

Appendix B
Proof of
theorem 1.1

The proof follows the one given in
[
38
]
with some similar notation. Let us first simplify the notation of the elements of
\Xi
by writing,

\xi(a|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a,\quad\xi(b|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}b\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\xi(c|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}c,

(B.1)

so that (
A.15
) is written,

f_{\vee}(a,f_{\vee}(b,c))=f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(a,b),c).

(B.2)

To simplify further, we will sometimes let,

f_{\vee}(a,b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a\circ b,

(B.3)

which then reduces (
A.15
) to,

(a\circ b)\circ c=a\circ(b\circ c).

(B.4)

Since
f_{\vee}
is a map to
\Xi
,
f_{\vee}:\Xi\times\Xi\rightarrow\Xi
, it suffices to determine its behavior in several different situations. This includes when both arguments are the same,
f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)
, which we will investigate first. We will then analyze the limits of the interval
\langle\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
, to determine if it is open or closed on either end.

Behavior of
f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)
—

There are three possible types of behavior of
f_{\vee}
for each individual element
\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}

\displaystyle f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)>\ell,

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)=\ell,

(B.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)<\ell.

(B.7)

Each element
\ell\in\Xi
will satisfy either of these equations for
f_{\vee}
, i.e. each element will either return itself in disjunction, or an element
\ell^{\prime}\in\Xi
which is a smaller or larger degree of belief than
\ell
. For each of these situations, we have the following consequences,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(\ell,a)>a,

(B.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(\ell,a)=a,

(B.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(\ell,a)<a.

(B.10)

For the disjunction function
f_{\vee}
, we necessarily have the situation
\mathbf{E}
for every element in
\Xi
62
62

62

This fact is quite subtle and one should use caution when analyzing
f_{\vee}
. Since
f_{\vee}
is defined as the
representation
of
\xi(a\vee b|\Gamma)
, its behavior necessarily depends on the behavior of
\vee
. For example, we have the identity
a\vee a=a
, thus it should be the case that for every
a\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
,
\xi(a\vee a)=\xi(a)
and hence
f_{\vee}(\xi(a),\xi(a))=\xi(a)
. However,
a
is not mutually exclusive from itself, so the result
f_{\vee}(\xi(a),\xi(a))=\xi(a)
only follows from the fact that the other two arguments,
\xi(a|a)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
, are constants and hence
f_{\vee}(\xi(a),\xi(a),\xi(a|a),\xi(a|a))\rightarrow f_{\vee}(\xi(a),\xi(a),\xi_{\mathbf{T}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}})
reduces in form to the mutually exclusive case.
. It will suffice however, to discuss the behavior of the other two situations. The first,
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
is easily shown from the associative property of
f_{\vee}
and
\mathbf{G}
,

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(\ell,f_{\vee}(\ell,a))

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell),a)

\displaystyle>f_{\vee}(\ell,a),

(B.11)

so that
f_{\vee}(\ell,a)>a
63
63

63

As shown in
[
38
]
, one can determine
\mathbf{G}
and likewise
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
if there exists at least one element
a_{0}\in\Xi
such that
f_{\vee}(\ell,a_{0})>a_{0}
. To see this, consider the associative property,

f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell),a_{0})=f_{\vee}(\ell,f_{\vee}(\ell,a_{0}))>a_{0}.

(B.12)

This implies that
f_{\vee}(\ell,\ell)>\ell
which is
\mathbf{G}
. Hence, a single
a_{0}
with this property is enough to impose
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
.
. Property
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
follows by analogy. The property
\mathbf{E}
identifies
\ell=e
as the identity element with respect to
f_{\vee}
. Thus,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
can only be true for one element of
\Xi
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:!\exists e\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(e,a)=a

.

(B.13)

Specifying the left limit
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
—

Depending on the existence of elements which have either property
\mathbf{G}
or
\mathbf{L}
, determines the type of interval
\langle\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
is with respect to its end points. Both end points will either be open or closed. First we study the behavior of the left side of the interval, for which there are three possible behaviors,

1.

Case 1
- The interval
[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
is closed on the left. Since
f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})\in[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
, the situation
\mathbf{L}
is impossible for
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
. Thus, we must either have,

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})>\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.14)

2.

Case 2
- The interval
(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
is open on the left, however we have the left hand limit
64
64

64

The existence of the left hand limit of course depends on whether
f_{\vee}
remains continuous and associative, however it must also allow for the reduction of
f_{\vee}(a,\ell)=f_{\vee}(c,\ell)
to
a=c
. This is easily seen by the continuity of
f_{\vee}
,

\displaystyle f_{\vee}(a,\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,b))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(a,\ell),b)=f_{\vee}(\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(a,\ell),b)

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}(\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(c,\ell),b)=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(c,f_{\vee}(\ell,b))

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}(c,\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,b)).

(B.15)

,

\displaystyle\mathbf{OG}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,a)

\displaystyle\geq a

(B.16)

\displaystyle>\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.17)

3.

Case 3
- The interval
(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
is open on the left, however we have the left hand limit
65
65

65

In this case, the left hand limit
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
plays the role of zero in multiplication. This comes immediately from
\mathbf{OL}
since,

\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}\left[\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,a)\right]\leq\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}a=\xi_{\mathbf{F}},

(B.18)

which means that,

\forall a,b\in\Xi:\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}\lim_{b\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(a,b)=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.19)

It is easy to show that for any element
a\in\Xi
we must then have,

\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,b)=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.20)

If one allowed for example,
\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,c)<a
, then we could conclude from
\mathbf{OL}
and (
B.19
),

\displaystyle a>\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,a)

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,\lim_{b\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(b,c))=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}\lim_{b\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,f_{\vee}(b,c))

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}\lim_{b\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(f_{\vee}(\ell,b),c)=\lim_{d\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(d,c)=a,

(B.21)

which is obviously a contradiction.
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{OL}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,a)<a.

(B.22)

In the first two cases,
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{OG}
, there are no inverse elements, however there can exist an identity. In
\mathbf{OG}
there is only an identity element if,

f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\xi_{\mathbf{F}},

(B.23)

so that
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}=e
. If this fails so that
f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})>\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
, then by the corollary in (
B.12
) we have for all
a\in\Xi
,
f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},a)>a
and hence for all
b\in\Xi
,
f_{\vee}(a,b)>a
. Thus there can be no identity for the system with
f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{F}})>\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
. If on the other hand (
B.23
) holds, then we have,

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(a,b)\geq f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}},b)

\displaystyle=b

\displaystyle>\xi_{\mathbf{F}},

(B.24)

which precludes the existence of inverse elements even for (
B.23
). For case three on the other hand, we can have inverses so that
f_{\vee}(a,a^{-1})=e
. This requires
e\neq\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
so that,

\exists a,c\in\Xi:a<e<c.

(B.25)

It is obvious that this cannot be the case for (
2.41
) since we have the identity
\mathbf{F}\vee\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
, which invokes (
B.23
) must be true. Therefore, with respect to (
2.41
) there can be no inverses of any element
\xi(a|\Gamma)\in\Xi
with respect to the disjunction
f_{\vee}
, however
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
plays the role of the identity. This also precludes case three, since we have the identity
a\vee\mathbf{F}=a
, therefore the inequality
\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{F}}}f_{\vee}(\ell,a)<a
cannot be satisfied. For the remainder of the proof, one can either adopt case one or two.

The recursive functions
\varphi_{m}
and
\psi
—

To continue, let us define the recursive functions
\varphi_{m}(a)
where,

\forall a\in\Xi:\varphi_{1}(a)=a\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\varphi_{m+1}(a)=f_{\vee}(a,\varphi_{m}(a)),

(B.26)

where
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This evaluates a set of
m
-nested disjunctions with respect to the element
a\in\Xi
. From the definition in (
B.26
) and the associativity property (
A.15
), we have,

\forall a\in\Xi:f_{\vee}(\varphi_{m_{1}}(a),\varphi_{m_{2}}(a))=f_{\vee}(\varphi_{m_{2}}(a),\varphi_{m_{1}}(a))=\varphi_{m_{1}+m_{2}}(a),

(B.27)

which using the
\circ
notation is simply,

\forall a\in\Xi:\varphi_{m_{1}+m_{2}}(a)=\varphi_{m_{1}}(a)\circ\varphi_{m_{2}}(a)=\varphi_{m_{2}}(a)\circ\varphi_{m_{1}}(a).

(B.28)

Likewise we have,

\forall a\in\Xi:\varphi_{m}\left[\varphi_{n}(a)\right]=\varphi_{n}\left[\varphi_{m}(a)\right]=\varphi_{mn}(a),

(B.29)

which means that
\varphi_{m}(a)
plays the role of multiplication with respect to
f_{\vee}
. If we have for each
a\in\Xi
,
f_{\vee}(a,a)>a
, then it follows that,

\varphi_{m+1}(a)>\varphi_{m}(a),

(B.30)

so long as
a\neq e
. Since we are excluding the possibility of
f_{\vee}(a,a)<a
, then we must have,

\varphi_{m+1}(a)\geq\varphi_{m}(a),

(B.31)

in general. Thus we can take the limit to the right part of the interval
[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
so that,

\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\varphi_{m}(a)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.32)

Since in general we have that
f_{\vee}
is increasing in both of its arguments,

\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}f_{\vee}(a,b)\geq\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}a=\xi_{\mathbf{T}},

(B.33)

which means that we must have,

\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}f_{\vee}(a,b)=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\lim_{b\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}f_{\vee}(a,b)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.34)

This shows that (
B.32
) must hold since,

\displaystyle\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}\varphi_{m}(a)

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}a\circ\varphi_{m-1}(a))

\displaystyle=\dots=\lim_{a\rightarrow\xi_{\mathbf{T}}}a\circ\dots\circ\varphi_{1}(a)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.35)

We also have for
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
,

\varphi_{m}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}\circ\varphi_{m-1}(\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=\dots=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.36)

Thus, due to (
B.36
) and (
B.26
), for every
a\in\Xi
the function
\varphi_{m}(a)
has a unique inverse,

\forall a\in\Xi:\forall m\in\mathbb{N}:\exists b\in\Xi:b=\varphi_{m}(a)\Rightarrow a=\varphi^{-1}(b),

(B.37)

where the inverse satisfies,

\varphi_{m}(a)\leq b<\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.38)

Thus the inverse function
b\in\Xi
is continuous and increasing in
a
. Now, let us associate to the function
a\in\Xi
the function
\psi
whose input is a rational number
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,

\psi\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\varphi_{n}^{-1}\left[\varphi_{m}(a)\right],

(B.39)

where
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
66
66

66

If one adopts the situation
\mathbf{G}
(
B.5
), then the definition (
B.39
) only applies for
m>n
.
. Since we have in particular property
\mathbf{E}
,
f_{\vee}(a,a)=a
for all
a\in\Xi
, the action of
\varphi
and its inverse
\varphi^{-1}
commute so that the above can be rewritten as,

\psi\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)=\varphi_{m}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right].

(B.40)

Naturally, the value
m=1
returns the original element,

\forall a\in\Xi:\psi(1)=a.

(B.41)

Since
\Xi
contains the identity
\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
, we can further define,

\psi(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\xi_{\mathbf{F}}.

(B.42)

From (
B.31
) and (
B.38
) the function
\psi
is strictly monotonically increasing,

\psi\left(\frac{m+1}{n}\right)=\varphi_{m+1}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right]>\varphi_{m}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right]=\psi\left(\frac{m}{n}\right).

(B.43)

Specifying the right limit
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
—

It suffices to discuss now the right side of the interval
[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
. In
[
38
]
it is assumed that the end point
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
is obtainable by an at most infinite limit,

\forall a\in\Xi:\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\psi(m)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.44)

In particular, if this limit exists, the point
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
cannot be within the interval. Thus, the right hand side must be open,
\langle\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}})
. One can easily show this by assuming the opposite. If for some
a\neq\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
we have,

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathbf{T}}<f_{\vee}(a,\xi_{\mathbf{T}})

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}\left(a,\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\psi(m)\right)=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}f_{\vee}(a,\varphi(a))

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{m+1}(a)=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\psi(m+1)=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}.

(B.45)

Thus we must have
f_{\vee}(a,\xi_{\mathbf{T}})=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
, which is certainly true since
\forall a\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:a\vee\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}
. While this certainly demonstrates that
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
cannot be in the interval
\langle\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle
if it exists as a limit point for repeated application of the disjunction, the limit in (
B.44
) is only true for
a=\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
. Since
f_{\vee}(\xi_{\mathbf{T}},a)
is never greater than
a
or
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
, the point
\xi_{\mathbf{T}}
can belong to the interval without generating a contradiction. Thus we have that the interval can be closed on both sides without loss of generality,
\langle\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}\rangle\rightarrow[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}]
.

The general solution —

Given the nature of (
B.43
)
67
67

67

As discussed in
[
38
]
, the equation (
B.46
) also holds for the property
\mathbf{G}
, however the derivation is more difficult.
, we can show the following,

\displaystyle f_{\vee}\left(\frac{}{}\psi\left(\frac{m_{1}}{n}\right),\psi\left(\frac{m_{2}}{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{m_{1}}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right]\circ\varphi_{m_{2}}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right]

\displaystyle=\varphi_{m_{1}+m_{2}}\left[\varphi_{n}^{-1}(a)\right]=\psi\left(\frac{m_{1}+m_{2}}{n}\right).

(B.46)

The function
\psi
is necessarily monotonic for all positive rational values
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
and hence possesses left and right limits for any positive real number
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
68
68

68

To see this, first consider that the converse is true via the limits being different,

r=\psi(x-0)<\psi(x+0)=s,

(B.47)

such that we have,

f_{\vee}(r,r)<f_{\vee}(r,s)<f_{\vee}(s,s),\quad\mathrm{or}\quad r<f_{\vee}(r,s)<s.

(B.48)

Since
f_{\vee}(r,s)
is increasing, and due to the strict inequalities above, we can always find an
\varepsilon
such that
f_{\vee}(r,r)+\varepsilon<f_{\vee}(r,s)<f_{\vee}(s,s)-\varepsilon
. Then, using continuity again we have the associated
\delta
’s to the
\varepsilon
that define
|r^{\prime}-r|<\delta
and
|s^{\prime}-s|<\delta
so that,

f_{\vee}(r,s)-\varepsilon<f_{\vee}(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})<f_{\vee}(r,s)+\varepsilon.

(B.49)

Using the definition of
r
and
s
in (
B.47
), we could always define
r^{\prime}=\psi(m_{r})
and
s^{\prime}=\psi(m_{s})
in terms of the rational numbers
m_{r}
and
m_{s}
where
m_{r}<x<m_{s}
and
\frac{m_{r}+m_{s}}{2}>x
. However, this would suggest that,

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}\left[\psi\left(\frac{m_{r}+m_{s}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\varphi_{2}\left[\varphi_{2}^{-1}\left[\varphi_{m_{r}+m_{s}}(a)\right]\right]=\psi(m_{r}+m_{s})=f_{\vee}(\psi(m_{r}),\psi(m_{s}))

\displaystyle=f_{\vee}(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})<f_{\vee}(r,s)+\varepsilon<f_{\vee}(s,s)=\varphi_{2}\left[\psi(x+0)\right],

(B.50)

which is a contradiction since
\varphi
is increasing.
Thus, the function
\psi(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
must be continuous. Finally, from (
B.26
) we have in the limit,

\forall x,y\in[\xi_{\mathbf{F}},\xi_{\mathbf{T}}]:\psi(x+y)=f_{\vee}(\psi(x),\psi(y)).

(B.51)

Substituting
a=\psi(x)
and
b=\psi(y)
we find,

f_{\vee}(a,b)=\psi\left(\psi^{-1}(a)+\psi^{-1}(b)\right).

(B.52)

Identifying
\phi=\psi^{-1}
reduces (
B.52
) to (
2.41
).

Appendix C
Proof of
theorem 1.2

We will first show the solution for rational numbers
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Consider that
\Xi
maps to the rational numbers,
\xi:\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
, so that the function
f_{\wedge}:\Xi\times\Xi\rightarrow\Xi
is a map from
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
to
\mathbb{Q}
. For ease of notation, let

\xi(a|\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a,\quad\xi(b|\Gamma\wedge a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}b\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\xi(c|\Gamma\wedge a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}c,

(C.1)

so that (
2.68
) is written,

f_{\wedge}(a,b+c)=f_{\wedge}(a,b)+f_{\wedge}(a,c).

(C.2)

Since
\Xi
is bounded by
[0,\xi_{\mathbf{T}}]
, it suffices to show that
f_{\wedge}
is linear in its second argument for two cases,
(b+c)=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}=0
and
(b+c)>0
. For the first case, consider
c=\xi_{\mathbf{F}}
,

\displaystyle f_{\wedge}(a,b+\xi_{\mathbf{F}})

\displaystyle=f_{\wedge}(a,b)+f_{\wedge}(a,\xi_{\mathbf{F}})

\displaystyle=f_{\wedge}(a,b).

(C.3)

Thus we have that
f_{\wedge}(a,\xi_{\mathbf{F}})=f_{\wedge}(a,0)=0
. Likewise, consider the repeated application of the Cauchy equation so that,

f_{\wedge}(a,b+\dots+b)=f_{\wedge}(a,nb)=nf_{\wedge}(a,b),

(C.4)

where
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Multiplying the right hand side of the above by
\frac{m}{n}
, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and substituting for
b
,
\frac{b}{n}
, we find,

mf_{\wedge}\left(a,\frac{b}{n}\right)=\frac{m}{n}f_{\wedge}(a,b).

(C.5)

Using the definition in (
C.4
), the left hand side of the above becomes,

f_{\wedge}\left(a,\frac{m}{n}b\right)=\frac{m}{n}f_{\wedge}(a,b).

(C.6)

Since
\frac{m}{n}
is rational, we have that,

f_{\wedge}(a,qb)=qf_{\wedge}(a,b),

(C.7)

for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Thus, for
b=1
we have,

f_{\wedge}(a,q)=qf_{\wedge}(a,1)=\lambda qg_{\wedge}(a),

(C.8)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, since
\Xi
is continuous, then the result will hold for any real number. Assume that
b
can be achieved as the limit of a sequence of rational numbers,

b=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}q_{k}.

(C.9)

Then, since
f_{\wedge}
and
\Xi
are continuous, the limit commutes between continuous maps,

\displaystyle f_{\wedge}(a,b)=f_{\wedge}(a,\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}q_{k})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}f_{\wedge}(a,q_{k})

\displaystyle=\lambda\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}q_{k}f_{\wedge}(a,1)=\lambda bf_{\wedge}(a,1).

(C.10)

Hence we have that,

\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R}:f_{\wedge}(a,b)=\lambda g_{\wedge}(a)b.

(C.11)

Appendix D
Proof of
theorem 3.1

Proof.

This proof follows the one given in Aczél
[
38
]
(see pgs. 141-142). We wish to show that the many-variable Pexider equation,

f(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},\dots,x_{n}+y_{n})=g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})+h(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n}),

(D.1)

reduces to a Cauchy type equation. First consider the special case when

y_{1}=y_{2}=\dots=y_{n}=0,

(D.2)

so that

f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})+h(0,0,\dots,0).

(D.3)

The value
h(0,0,\dots,0)=a
with
a
a constant. Likewise for

x_{1}=x_{2}=\dots=x_{n}=0,

(D.4)

we have,

f(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})=g(0,0,\dots,0)+h(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n}),

(D.5)

where
g(0,0,\dots,0)=b
with
b
another constant. Thus we have,

\displaystyle g(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})-a,

(D.6)

\displaystyle h(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})

\displaystyle=f(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})-b.

(D.7)

The many-variable Pexider equation can then be written as,

\displaystyle f(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},\dots,x_{n}+y_{n})

\displaystyle=f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle+f(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})-a-b.

(D.8)

Letting

\xi(t)=f(t)-a-b,

(D.9)

eq. (
D.8
) becomes,

\xi(x_{1}+y_{1},\dots,x_{n}+y_{n})=\xi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})+\xi(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),

(D.10)

which is the many-variable Cauchy equation.
∎
